<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the isoperimetric conjecture for the cubic flat three-torus and classify all minimizers, including the equality cases at the transition volumes $`4\pi/81`$ and $`1/\pi`$. The minimizing regions are balls, circular tubes about shortest closed geodesics, coordinate slabs, and their complements.

- **claim-cubic-torus-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-intro-main)): For $V\in(0,1)$, put $v=\min(V,1-V)$. Among finite-perimeter subsets of $\mathbb R^3/\mathbb Z^3$ of volume $V$, the minimum perimeter is $$I_{\rm unit}(V)= \min\left\{(36\pi)^{1/3}v^{2/3},\ 2\sqrt{\pi v},\ 2\right\}. \label{intro:profile}$$ For $0<V\le1/2$, every minimizing region is, up to an ambient isometry and a null set, one of the following: $$\begin{array}{c\|c} \text{volume range}&\text{region}\\ \hline 0<V\le 4\pi/81 & \text{a ball of radius }(3V/(4\pi))^{1/3}\\[2pt] 4\pi/81\le V\le1/\pi &\text{a solid circular tube of radius }\sqrt{V/\pi}\\ &\text{about a shortest closed geodesic}\\[2pt] 1/\pi\le V\le1/2 &\text{a slab of width }V\text{ between coordinate tori}. \end{array}$$ Both listed types occur at each common endpoint of adjacent ranges, and there are no further equality cases. For $V>1/2$, the minimizing regions are the complements of those at volume $1-V$. At zero or full volume the conclusion is trivial.
  - Explanation: Determines the minimum perimeter at every volume and classifies all minimizing regions, including transition ties and complements.
- **claim-flat-boundary-structure** (classification, [source](#stmt-pre-structure)): Let $E$ be an isoperimetric region in a complete orientable flat three-manifold, with closed boundary $\Sigma$. Either $\Sigma$ is a union of parallel planar surfaces, or $\Sigma$ is connected. In the connected case:* 1. *genus zero gives a round sphere;* 2. *genus one gives a flat quotient of a plane or a circular cylinder;* 3. *the genus is at most four;* 4. *in genus two or three, $H^2\|\Sigma\|\le 2\pi$, where $H=h/2$; in genus four, $H=0$.
  - Explanation: Restricts minimizing boundaries to parallel planar surfaces or connected surfaces with controlled genus and curvature.
- **claim-standard-competitors** (classification, [source](#stmt-pre-standard)): The function $F$ is attained by standard competitors. Every standard minimizing boundary has $A\ge F(V)$; equality gives exactly the balls, shortest-geodesic tubes, and coordinate slabs in Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>, including the indicated ties and complements.
  - Explanation: Shows that standard competitors attain the candidate envelope and identifies the standard equality cases.
- **claim-profile-upper-supports** (theorem, [source](#stmt-pre-supports)): The profile $I:[0,1]\to[0,\infty)$ is continuous, concave, and satisfies $I(V)=I(1-V)$. If a minimizer at $V\in(0,1)$ has sum curvature $h$, there is a smooth local upper support $C$ for $I$ at $V$ such that $C'(V)=h$ and $C''(V)\le0$. If its boundary is connected of genus $g$, then at contact $$C^2C''=-h^2C+\pi(1-g). \label{pre:parallel}$$ Every such contact curvature is nonnegative for $V<1/2$ and nonpositive for $V>1/2$.
  - Explanation: Establishes profile continuity, concavity and complement symmetry, and relates upper-support derivatives to curvature and genus.
- **claim-transition-volume-reduction** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-pre-reduction)): If every minimizer at $V_1$ and $V_2$ is standard, then $I=F$ on $[0,1]$, and every minimizer is one of the standard regions of Lemma <a href="#pre:standard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:standard">3</a>.
  - Explanation: Reduces the complete profile and equality classification to standardness of every minimizer at the transition volumes.
- **claim-coordinate-reflection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-reflection)): After translating the coordinate origins, the original region $E$ is invariant under reflection in each of the six coordinate tori $x_i=0,1$.
  - Explanation: For a nonstandard minimizing boundary, coordinate translations make the original occupied region invariant under the coordinate reflections.
- **claim-strict-coordinate-monotonicity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-normals)): After possibly reversing individual coordinates in $Q$, $$N_i>0\qquad\text{on }\Sigma\text{ whenever }0<x_i<1. \label{geo:strict-normal}$$ Consequently $E\cap Q$ is a down-set for the coordinatewise order.
  - Explanation: Uses stability to fix strict signs of the interior normal components and place the occupied region in down-set position.
- **claim-admissible-vertex-patterns** (classification, [source](#stmt-geo-patterns)): No vertex of $Q$ lies on $\Sigma$. Up to taking the complementary region and reversing all coordinates, and up to coordinate permutations, the only possible vertex sets for a nonstandard minimizer are $$\{000,100,010\},\qquad \{000,100,010,001\}. \label{geo:two-patterns}$$ The four-vertex pattern may be placed on the smaller-volume side. No such assertion is yet made for the three-vertex pattern.
  - Explanation: Leaves only the three-vertex and four-vertex occupied patterns for a nonstandard minimizer, with a smaller-volume-side choice established only for the latter.
- **claim-slice-regularity-substitution** (theorem, [source](#stmt-slice-regularity)): There are only finitely many exceptional axial heights. The function $v$ is continuous, nonincreasing, absolutely continuous, and smooth off those heights. The end levels are regular, with a regular collar whenever nonempty. For every nonnegative measurable function $\gamma$, $$\int_0^1 \gamma(v(z))(-v'(z))\,dz =\int_r^s\gamma(w)\,dw. \label{slice:substitution}$$
  - Explanation: Controls exceptional heights and proves absolute continuity of section area, allowing integration in the section-area coordinate.
- **claim-slice-flux-projection-coarea** (theorem, [source](#stmt-slice-identities)): With the preceding notation, $$\begin{aligned} \int_{\partial E_z}\alpha\,d\ell &=b_z A+h(v(z)-V),\notag\\ p(0)&=b_zA+h(s-V),& p(1)&=b_zA+h(r-V), \label{slice:flux}\\ s-r&=\int_{\Sigma\cap Q}N_z\,dS \le A\sqrt{1-b_z}, \label{slice:projection}\\ -v'(z)&=\int_{\partial E_z}\frac{N_z}{\alpha}\,d\ell,& A&=\int_0^1\int_{\partial E_z}\frac1\alpha\,d\ell\,dz. \label{slice:coarea} \end{aligned}$$ Moreover $b_x+b_y+b_z=2$, and $b_i<1$ in every direction.
  - Explanation: Relates section perimeter and area variation to curvature, normal projections and total boundary area.
- **claim-four-vertex-exclusion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-slice-four-vertex)): A nonstandard minimizer at either $V_1=4\pi/81$ or $V_2=1/\pi$ cannot have the occupied vertex pattern $\{000,100,010,001\}$.
  - Explanation: Excludes the four-vertex candidate at both transition volumes by reducing its section inequalities to an impossible scalar condition.
- **claim-first-transition-three-vertex-exclusion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-slice-three-first)): A nonstandard minimizer at $V_1=4\pi/81$ cannot have the three-vertex pattern.
  - Explanation: Excludes the three-vertex candidate at the ball–tube transition using bottom-section flux and projection bounds.
- **claim-second-transition-parameter-window** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-slice-three-second-parameters)): Suppose that a nonstandard minimizer at $V_2=1/\pi$ has the three-vertex pattern. Then $A\le1$, the identities <a href="#slice:three-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-flux">[slice:three-flux]</a> hold, and $$\label{slice:three-parameters} \frac{\sqrt{\pi(1-s)}}s\le h\le\pi(1-s^2),\qquad 0.535<s<0.89,\qquad 0.64\le h\le2.25,\qquad s\ge t(h),$$ where $$\label{slice:t-definition} t=t(h):=\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{1+4h^2/\pi}}, \qquad h^2t^2=\pi(1-t).$$
  - Explanation: Confines any remaining three-vertex candidate at the tube–slab transition to explicit area and curvature ranges.
- **claim-integrated-flux-comparison** (bound, [source](#stmt-nest-integrated-flux)): At every nonempty regular slice, with area $v$ and perimeter $p$, $$\label{nest:jensen} \int_{\partial E_z}\frac{d\ell}{\alpha}-\lambda hv \geq G(hv/p)\int_{\partial E_z} \frac{\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}}{\alpha}\,d\ell.$$ If a nonnegative measurable function $\gamma$ satisfies $G(hv(z)/p(z))\geq\gamma(v(z))$ at the nonempty regular levels, then $$\label{nest:area-coordinate} A\geq\frac{\lambda h}{\pi}+\int_0^s\gamma(v)\,dv.$$
  - Explanation: Converts the arclength mean imposed by flux into a whole-slice inequality and then an integrated lower bound for surface area.
- **claim-planar-baseline-slack** (bound, [source](#stmt-nest-planar-patterns)): Every nonempty regular slice satisfies $$\label{nest:planar-bound} p\geq p_*(v):= \min\{\sqrt{\pi v},\ 1,\ \sqrt{\pi(1-v)}\}.$$ Put $a_0=1/\pi$, $b_0=1-1/\pi$, and define $$\label{nest:baseline} B(h)=\frac{\lambda h}{\pi} +\int_0^{t(h)}G\!\left(\frac{hv}{p_*(v)}\right)\,dv-1.$$ At $v=0$ the argument in the integrand is understood by its limit. Then $$\label{nest:baseline-slack} A-1\geq B(h)+\ell(s-t).$$
  - Explanation: Combines the planar corner, strip and complementary-corner bounds into a baseline area estimate retaining bottom-area slack.
- **claim-bottom-extent-calibration** (bound, [source](#stmt-nest-bottom-calibration)): Each $L\in\{L_x,L_y\}$ satisfies $$\label{nest:extent-bound} hs\geq\frac{1-s}{L}+\frac{\pi L}{4}.$$ Consequently, a strip slice, written as a decreasing subgraph across either square coordinate, has smaller endpoint at most $$\label{nest:endpoint-bound} \begin{split} e&=1-\frac2\pi \left(hs-\sqrt{h^2s^2-\pi(1-s)}\right)\\ &\leq1-\frac{2ht}{\pi}+QY, \qquad Y=\sqrt{s-t},\qquad Q=\frac2\pi\sqrt{\pi+2h^2t}. \end{split}$$
  - Explanation: Calibrates both bottom-complement extents and uses containment to constrain the smaller endpoint of a strip in either orientation.
- **claim-nested-slice-perimeter-gain** (bound, [source](#stmt-nest-shape-gain)): On $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$ one has $$\label{nest:interval-placement} u-d_m\geq\frac{1033}{2700}>0.38>a_0, \qquad u\leq b_0,\qquad u<t\leq s,\qquad hu\leq0.96.$$ Define $$\label{nest:shape-function} H(q)=\min\left\{q^2,\frac{\pi(1-q)}{c d_m}\right\}, \qquad 0\leq q\leq1.$$ If $\delta>0$, put $q=(v-u+\delta)/\delta$ for $u-\delta\leq v\leq u$. Every regular slice in this area interval satisfies $$\label{nest:improved-perimeter} p^2\geq1+c\delta^2H(q).$$
  - Explanation: Places the gain interval inside the planar middle branch and proves a common perimeter improvement for every possible slice type.
- **claim-weight-derivative-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-nest-derivative-bound)): For $0\leq X\leq0.96$, $$\label{nest:derivative-inequality} \frac{0.48-0.49X^2}{(1-X^2)^{3/2}}\geq0.91X.$$
  - Explanation: Provides the derivative lower bound needed to turn perimeter improvement into an increase in the flux weight.
- **claim-uniform-shape-coefficient** (bound, [source](#stmt-nest-coefficient-bound)): The constants in <a href="#nest:shape-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:shape-moments">[nest:shape-moments]</a> satisfy $$\label{nest:moment-bounds} M>0.2659,\qquad q_m>0.697,\qquad p_2<1.09.$$ For $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$, $$\label{nest:coefficient-inequality} \frac{0.91cM}{p_2^{3/2}} \bigl[h(u-(1-q_m)d_m)\bigr]^3 \geq C_0(h),$$ where $$\label{nest:gain-coefficient} C_0(h)=\min\{0.134h^3,0.36\}.$$
  - Explanation: Bounds the shape moments and derives a uniform coefficient for the integrated cubic gain.
- **claim-nesting-area-defect** (bound, [source](#stmt-nest-area-bound)): For the three-vertex configuration at $V=1/\pi$ with $A\leq1$, $$A-1\geq B(h)\qquad(0.64\leq h\leq2.25).$$ On $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$ define $$\label{nest:gain-definition} S(h)=\inf_{y\geq0} \left\{\ell y^2+ C_0(h)\min\{d_m,(P-Qy)_+\}^{\,3}\right\}.$$ Then $$\label{nest:area-gain} A-1\geq B(h)+S(h).$$
  - Explanation: Combines the baseline, bottom-area slack and nested perimeter gain, then minimizes over the unknown slack.
- **claim-four-vertex-scalar-obstruction** (bound, [source](#stmt-num-four-vertex-scalar)): Suppose $$\begin{gathered} 0<d\le\frac1{\sqrt3},\qquad 0\le x\le\sqrt\pi,\qquad 0\le\tau<1,\\ \tau+\frac{x}{\pi}(1-\tau^2)=1-d^2. \end{gathered}$$ Then $$d+\frac{\tau^2+(\tau+xd)^2}{\pi}<1.$$
  - Explanation: Proves the strict scalar inequality contradicting the necessary condition derived from a four-vertex candidate.
- **claim-scalar-defect-positivity** (bound, [source](#stmt-num-scalar-positive)): The following strict inequalities hold: $$\begin{aligned} B(h)&>0 &&\text{for }h\in[.64,1.02]\cup[1.62,2.25],\\ B(h)+S(h)&>0 &&\text{for }h\in[1.02,1.62]. \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Certifies positivity of the baseline outside the central curvature interval and of the nesting-enhanced defect inside it.
- **claim-layer-cake-semiconcavity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-num-layer-cake)): For $.64\le h\le2.25$, $$\label{num:layer-cake-formula} B(h)+1=\frac{\lambda h}{\pi}+\ell t+ \int_0^D W(X) \min\left\{\pi(X/h)^2,\ X/h,\ \rho(X/h)\right\}\,dX.$$ On an interval $[a,b]\subset[.64,2.25]$, the function $B(h)-h^2/a^2$ is concave.
  - Explanation: Represents the baseline by weighted sublevel lengths and controls its upper second derivative through formula changes.
- **claim-gain-semiconcavity** (bound, [source](#stmt-num-s-second)): The function $S$ has upper second derivative bound $.4$ on $[1.02,1.62]$.
  - Explanation: Controls the gain's upper second derivative across cap changes, parameter switches and changes of minimizing slack.
- **claim-baseline-taylor-enclosure** (bound, [source](#stmt-num-taylor-error)): For $.64\le h\le2.25$, $$\label{num:taylor-bound} B(h)\ge\widetilde B(h)-.008(D/h-b_0)_+^4.$$
  - Explanation: Bounds the error from replacing the active inverse-profile branch by a midpoint cubic Taylor polynomial.
- **claim-capped-gain-evaluation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nest-s-formula)): On $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$, $P,Q,C_0$ are positive. With $$\label{nest:s-critical-point} q_*=\frac{2P}{1+\sqrt{1+6C_0Q^2P/\ell}},$$ one has, including every cap and zero case, $$\label{nest:s-explicit} S(h)=\min\left\{C_0d_m^3, \ell\left(\frac{P-q_*}{Q}\right)^2+C_0q_*^3\right\}.$$
  - Explanation: Evaluates the gain infimum by comparing the cap value with the uncapped critical-point value, including threshold cases.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-nest-integrated-flux",
        "exact_quote": "At every nonempty regular slice, with area $v$ and perimeter $p$, $$\\label{nest:jensen}\n \\int_{\\partial E_z}\\frac{d\\ell}{\\alpha}-\\lambda hv\n \\geq\n G(hv/p)\\int_{\\partial E_z}\n       \\frac{\\sqrt{1-\\alpha^2}}{\\alpha}\\,d\\ell.$$ If a nonnegative measurable function $\\gamma$ satisfies $G(hv(z)/p(z))\\geq\\gamma(v(z))$ at the nonempty regular levels, then $$\\label{nest:area-coordinate}\n A\\geq\\frac{\\lambda h}{\\pi}+\\int_0^s\\gamma(v)\\,dv.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-354.1"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Normalized period-two torus** ([source](#pre:section)): We work on $$T=\mathbb R^3/(2\mathbb Z^3), \qquad V(E)=\frac{\|E\|}{8}, \qquad A(E)=\frac{\|\partial E\|}{8}.$$
- **Sum curvature** ([source](#pre:section)): The outward unit normal is $N$, and $h=k_1+k_2$ is the sum of the principal curvatures in the metric of $T$, positive on a ball.
- **Standard minimizing boundary** ([source](#pre:curvature)): We call a minimizing boundary *standard* if it is planar, spherical, or a circular-cylinder quotient.
- **Candidate profile and transition volumes** ([source](#pre:envelope)): For $0\le V\le1$, set $v=\min(V,1-V)$ and $$F(V)=\min\left\{ (9\pi/2)^{1/3}v^{2/3},\ \sqrt{\pi v},\ 1 \right\}, \qquad V_1=\frac{4\pi}{81},\quad V_2=\frac1\pi. \label{pre:envelope}$$ The first, second, and third expressions are the respective minima on $0\le v\le V_1$, $V_1\le v\le V_2$, and $V_2\le v\le1/2$.
- **Planar sections and endpoint areas** ([source](#slice:section)): Choose an axial direction $z$ in the reflected cube $Q$, and write $$E_z=\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^2:(x,y,z)\in E\},\qquad v(z)=\|E_z\|,\quad s=v(0),\quad r=v(1).$$
- **Relative section perimeter** ([source](#slice:section)): Let $p(z)$ be the relative perimeter of $E_z$ in the square.
- **Surface coarea Jacobian** ([source](#slice:section)): The surface coarea Jacobian for the height function is $$\alpha=\|\nabla_\Sigma z\|=\sqrt{1-N_z^2}.$$
- **Mean square coarea factor** ([source](#slice:section)): Its mean square over the surface patch will be denoted by $$b_z=\frac1A\int_{\Sigma\cap Q}(1-N_z^2)\,dS.$$
- **Bottom-area threshold** ([source](#stmt-slice-three-second-parameters)): where $$\label{slice:t-definition} t=t(h):=\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{1+4h^2/\pi}}, \qquad h^2t^2=\pi(1-t).$$
- **Flux ratio** ([source](#pm-h-12)): At a constant value $0<\alpha<1$, their ratio is $g(\alpha)$, where $$g(X)=\frac{1-\lambda X^2}{\sqrt{1-X^2}},\qquad 0\leq X<1.$$
- **Non-increasing flux minorant** ([source](#nest:weight)): Set $$\label{nest:weight} \begin{gathered} D=\frac{\sqrt{48}}7,\qquad \ell=0.28,\\ G(X)=g(X_0),\qquad X_0=\min\{X,D\},\quad X\geq0. \end{gathered}$$
- **Planar perimeter lower envelope** ([source](#stmt-nest-planar-patterns)): Every nonempty regular slice satisfies $$\label{nest:planar-bound} p\geq p_*(v):= \min\{\sqrt{\pi v},\ 1,\ \sqrt{\pi(1-v)}\}.$$
- **Baseline area defect** ([source](#stmt-nest-planar-patterns)): Put $a_0=1/\pi$, $b_0=1-1/\pi$, and define $$\label{nest:baseline} B(h)=\frac{\lambda h}{\pi} +\int_0^{t(h)}G\!\left(\frac{hv}{p_*(v)}\right)\,dv-1.$$
- **Shape gain function** ([source](#stmt-nest-shape-gain)): Define $$\label{nest:shape-function} H(q)=\min\left\{q^2,\frac{\pi(1-q)}{c d_m}\right\}, \qquad 0\leq q\leq1.$$
- **Shape moments and distortion bound** ([source](#nest:shape-moments)): Define the moments and a perimeter bound associated with $H$ by $$\label{nest:shape-moments} M=\int_0^1H(q)\,dq,\qquad q_m=\frac{\int_0^1qH(q)\,dq}{M},\qquad p_2=1+c d_m^2\max_{[0,1]}H.$$
- **Optimized nesting gain** ([source](#stmt-nest-area-bound)): On $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$ define $$\label{nest:gain-definition} S(h)=\inf_{y\geq0} \left\{\ell y^2+ C_0(h)\min\{d_m,(P-Qy)_+\}^{\,3}\right\}.$$
- **Inverse complementary-corner profile** ([source](#num:rho)): Let $\rho$ be the inverse of the increasing function $r\mapsto r/\sqrt{\pi(1-r)}$ on $[0,1)$.
- **Upper second derivative bound** ([source](#pm-h-19)): We say that a function has upper second derivative bound $K$ on an interval if subtracting $Kh^2/2$ makes it concave there.

| q-gain-upper-second-derivative | Upper second derivative bound of S | = | $.4$ | Central curvature interval, with the bound understood through subtraction of a quadratic. |
| q-interpolation-uniform-margin | Smallest certified uniform margin | = | $9/100000$ | The interpolation intervals attaining the smallest stated margin. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-classical-structure** [claim](#stmt-pre-structure) / [proof](#pre:curvature): Apply the classical structure theorem with the identity symmetry group and translate its curvature estimate to the chosen normalization. -> Standard boundaries or connected higher-genus candidates satisfying the curvature-area bound.
- **step-standard-envelope** [claim](#stmt-pre-standard) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-standard): Compute areas and volumes of embedded balls and tubes, bound planar components by primitive lattice normals, and inspect equality. -> Attainment of the candidate envelope and classification of standard equality cases.
- **step-profile-supports** [claim](#stmt-pre-supports) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-supports): Use compactness, complementation, parallel displacement and Gauss–Bonnet; rule out failure of concavity by a touching quadratic. -> Concavity, symmetry, contact-curvature signs and the upper-support identity.
- **step-conditional-global-reduction** [claim](#stmt-pre-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-reduction): At a hypothetical negative minimum of the profile difference, compare the smooth candidate branch with an upper support and use the genus term for a contradiction. -> Conditional global profile equality and complete minimizer classification.
- **step-reflection-invariance** [claim](#stmt-geo-reflection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-reflection): Bisect occupied volume, reflect each half, and use minimality, connectedness and analytic continuation to recover the original region. -> Coordinate reflection invariance of the original occupied region.
- **step-normal-signs** [claim](#stmt-geo-normals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-normals): Apply constrained stability to nodal-domain restrictions of translation Jacobi fields, then use reflection and unique continuation to force strict signs. -> Strict coordinate monotonicity and down-set position.
- **step-vertex-patterns** [claim](#stmt-geo-patterns) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-patterns): Classify occupied vertex order ideals and exclude the remaining alternatives by radial parametrizations or disconnected reflected graphs. -> Only the three-vertex and four-vertex candidate patterns remain.
- **step-area-coordinate** [claim](#stmt-slice-regularity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-slice-regularity): Locate exceptional heights at isolated axial-edge intersections, eliminate singular variation of section area, and identify its pushforward measure. -> Absolute continuity and substitution from height to section area.
- **step-section-identities** [claim](#stmt-slice-identities) / [proof](#proof-stmt-slice-identities): Apply axial first variation, divergence, Cauchy–Schwarz and surface coarea, including regular endpoint collars. -> Flux, projection and coarea identities and the axial mean-square constraints.
- **step-four-vertex-scalar** [claim](#stmt-num-four-vertex-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-num-four-vertex-scalar): Split the scalar parameter range, use monotonicity in one part, and rescale the other part to compare explicit algebraic maxima. -> Strict scalar obstruction to the four-vertex necessary condition.
- **step-four-vertex-exclusion** [claim](#stmt-slice-four-vertex) / [proof](#proof-stmt-slice-four-vertex): Choose the slicing direction with a large mean-square coarea factor, combine endpoint corner bounds with flux and projection, and invoke the scalar obstruction. -> Exclusion of the four-vertex pattern at both transitions.
- **step-first-three-vertex-exclusion** [claim](#stmt-slice-three-first) / [proof](#proof-stmt-slice-three-first): Use the vacant top collar to determine the flux constant, establish the occupied-side choice, and contradict the resulting scalar bound on separate parameter ranges. -> Exclusion of the three-vertex pattern at the first transition.
- **step-second-transition-parameters** [claim](#stmt-slice-three-second-parameters) / [proof](#proof-stmt-slice-three-second-parameters): Combine bottom flux and projection, analyze the resulting unimodal area constraint, and solve the bottom perimeter quadratic. -> Compact curvature window and a lower threshold for bottom area.
- **step-integrated-flux** [claim](#stmt-nest-integrated-flux) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-integrated-flux): Prove convexity of the auxiliary slice function, apply Jensen with the flux-determined arclength mean, then integrate using the area coordinate. -> Whole-slice comparison and integrated area lower bound.
- **step-planar-baseline** [claim](#stmt-nest-planar-patterns) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-planar-patterns): Treat occupied corners, strips and complementary corners separately, substitute their common lower envelope into the non-increasing weight, and retain the bottom-area slack. -> Baseline defect plus a positive slack contribution.
- **step-bottom-calibration** [claim](#stmt-nest-bottom-calibration) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-bottom-calibration): Pair the complementary-corner tangent with a unit vector field, solve the extent inequality and use slice containment in both strip orientations. -> A common upper bound on the smaller endpoint of every strip orientation.
- **step-shape-gain** [claim](#stmt-nest-shape-gain) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-shape-gain): Place the gain interval inside the middle planar branch and prove the squared-perimeter improvement separately for occupied corners, complementary corners and calibrated strips. -> A common shape-dependent perimeter gain across slice-type changes.
- **step-weight-derivative** [claim](#stmt-nest-derivative-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-derivative-bound): Separate numerator and denominator monotonicity and verify rational bounds on the displayed argument intervals. -> Uniform derivative lower bound for converting perimeter gain to weight gain.
- **step-shape-coefficient** [claim](#stmt-nest-coefficient-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-coefficient-bound): Integrate the shape function on its branches, enclose the meeting point rationally, and bound the gain coefficient in both placement regimes. -> Uniform coefficient controlling the integrated cubic gain.
- **step-area-gain** [claim](#stmt-nest-area-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-area-bound): Integrate the weight improvement over the gain interval, apply Jensen to the cubic moment, add the baseline slack and minimize over the unknown bottom area. -> Baseline and nesting-enhanced lower bounds for the candidate area defect.
- **step-layer-cake-curvature** [claim](#stmt-num-layer-cake) / [proof](#proof-stmt-num-layer-cake): Use the weighted sublevel-set representation of the flux minorant, invert the planar branches, and control selected branch derivatives and their jumps. -> Layer-cake formula and semiconcavity of the baseline.
- **step-gain-curvature** [claim](#stmt-num-s-second) / [proof](#proof-stmt-num-s-second): Bound the second derivative of every fixed-slack branch, check nonpositive derivative jumps, and pass concavity through the infimum. -> Upper second derivative bound for the optimized gain.
- **step-taylor-error** [claim](#stmt-num-taylor-error) / [proof](#proof-stmt-num-taylor-error): Bound the inverse profile's fourth derivative on the active interval and integrate the midpoint Taylor remainder against the nonnegative weight. -> An explicit one-sided correction for the cubic Taylor evaluation.
- **step-capped-gain-formula** [claim](#stmt-nest-s-formula) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-s-formula): Change from slack to the affine endpoint variable, solve the strictly convex uncapped objective and compare all cap, zero and feasibility cases. -> Explicit gain evaluation using arithmetic and square roots.
- **step-certified-positivity** [claim](#stmt-num-scalar-positive) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-s-formula-2): Enclose knot values by outward arithmetic, subtract the Taylor correction, and extend their positivity over every interval by the semiconcavity chord bound. -> Strict defect positivity throughout the required curvature ranges.
- **step-complete-classification** [claim](#stmt-intro-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nest-area-bound-2): Exclude the remaining transition candidate by strict area-defect positivity, apply the conditional global reduction, and restore the unit-torus normalization. -> The unit cubic torus profile and all minimizing regions, including transition equality cases.

In the connected case:*\n\n1.  *genus zero gives a round sphere;*\n\n2.  *genus one gives a flat quotient of a plane or a circular cylinder;*\n\n3.  *the genus is at most four;*\n\n4.  *in genus two or three, $H^2|\\Sigma|\\le 2\\pi$, where $H=h/2$; in genus four, $H=0$.",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-rational-gap-certificates**: The rational interpolation margins dominate the integer lower bounds in the gap table and are strictly positive on every listed interpolation interval.
- **invariant-continuous-positivity-transfer**: Corrected endpoint bounds and upper second derivative bounds transfer the finite arithmetic certificates to strict positivity throughout the stated curvature ranges.
- **invariant-taylor-correction**: The prescribed rational error majorant accounts for the Taylor approximation before interpolation margins are formed.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-global-proof-from-arithmetic**: Treat successful finite arithmetic checks as an independent proof of the full geometric classification. -> The arithmetic accepts the knot lower bounds as inputs and does not establish reflection symmetry, stability, slicing identities, analytic derivative estimates, or the knot enclosures themselves.
- **anti-knot-sampling-alone**: Infer positivity between knots solely from positive sampled endpoint values. -> An uncontrolled interior dip could invalidate the inference.
- **anti-uncorrected-baseline**: Use the tabulated approximation to the baseline directly as a lower bound for the exact baseline. -> The Taylor approximation can overestimate the exact baseline.
- **anti-transfer-to-other-lattices**: Apply the classified regions and transition volumes to arbitrary periodic lattices. -> The ambient metric and coordinate geometry would differ from those in the theorem.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The finite constructive target is the rational comparison underlying the interpolation gap table. The source states: “Every entry in Table” and specifies verification “by rational cross-multiplication.” This checks the displayed rational margins from the supplied knot bounds; it does not certify the knot enclosures, analytic lemmas, or geometric classification.

```python
# INPUTS: A JSON object with integer interval in [0,22].
# PRECONDITIONS: The published knot lower bounds and analytic interpolation estimates are accepted as inputs.
# OUTPUTS: JSON integer numerator n and positive denominator d for the exact rational margin M.
# TIME: A fixed collection of rational operations per selected interval; integer arithmetic cost depends on operand size.
# SPACE: A fixed knot table and finitely many arbitrary-precision rational intermediates.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact rational arithmetic; decimal constants are parsed from strings.
# FAILURE: Invalid input raises an exception; no floating-point approximation or fallback is used.
import json
import sys
from fractions import Fraction as F

H = list(map(F, ['.64','.74','.84','.92','.98','1.02','1.06','1.10','1.14','1.18','1.22','1.26','1.30','1.34','1.38','1.42','1.46','1.50','1.54','1.58','1.62','1.70','1.90','2.25']))
B = [1744,1200,735,432,247,144,57,-15,-74,-119,-153,-178,-196,-211,-226,-247,-275,-260,-193,-76,86,536,2275,6853]
S = [None,None,None,None,None,76,98,123,151,182,215,247,271,297,325,332,332,318,293,271,251,None,None,None]
PI_LO = F('3.14159265358979')
D_HI = F('.989743318611')
ERROR_COEFF = F('.008')
INTERIOR_K = F('2.4')
EXTERIOR_NUM = F(2)

def error(h):
    b_lo = 1 - 1 / PI_LO
    return ERROR_COEFF * max(D_HI / h - b_lo, F(0)) ** 4

def margin(i):
    a, b = H[i], H[i + 1]
    interior = F('1.02') <= a and b <= F('1.62')
    k = INTERIOR_K if interior else EXTERIOR_NUM / (a * a)
    ends = []
    for j in (i, i + 1):
        gain = S[j] if interior else 0
        ends.append(F(B[j] + gain, 10**5) - error(H[j]))
    return min(ends) - k * (b - a) ** 2 / 8

obj = json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(obj, dict) or set(obj) != {'interval'}:
    raise ValueError('expected interval object')
i = obj['interval']
if type(i) is not int or not 0 <= i < len(H) - 1:
    raise ValueError('invalid interval')
r = margin(i)
print(json.dumps({'n': r.numerator, 'd': r.denominator}, sort_keys=True))
```

no floating-point approximation or fallback is used.\nimport json\nimport sys\nfrom fractions import Fraction as F\n\nH = list(map(F, ['.64','.74','.84','.92','.98','1.02','1.06','1.10','1.14','1.18','1.22','1.26','1.30','1.34','1.38','1.42','1.46','1.50','1.54','1.58','1.62','1.70','1.90','2.25']))\nB = [1744,1200,735,432,247,144,57,-15,-74,-119,-153,-178,-196,-211,-226,-247,-275,-260,-193,-76,86,536,2275,6853]\nS = [None,None,None,None,None,76,98,123,151,182,215,247,271,297,325,332,332,318,293,271,251,None,None,None]\nPI_LO = F('3.14159265358979')\nD_HI = F('.989743318611')\nERROR_COEFF = F('.008')\nINTERIOR_K = F('2.4')\nEXTERIOR_NUM = F(2)\n\ndef error(h):\n    b_lo = 1 - 1 / PI_LO\n    return ERROR_COEFF * max(D_HI / h - b_lo, F(0)) ** 4\n\ndef margin(i):\n    a, b = H[i], H[i + 1]\n    interior = F('1.02') <= a and b <= F('1.62')\n    k = INTERIOR_K if interior else EXTERIOR_NUM / (a * a)\n    ends = []\n    for j in (i, i + 1):\n        gain = S[j] if interior else 0\n        ends.append(F(B[j] + gain, 10**5) - error(H[j]))\n    return min(ends) - k * (b - a) ** 2 / 8\n\nobj = json.load(sys.stdin)\nif not isinstance(obj, dict) or set(obj) != {'interval'}:\n    raise ValueError('expected interval object')\ni = obj['interval']\nif type(i) is not int or not 0 <= i < len(H) - 1:\n    raise ValueError('invalid interval')\nr = margin(i)\nprint(json.dumps({'n': r.numerator, 'd': r.denominator}, sort_keys=True))\n",
    "test_vectors": [

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The explicit perimeter profile and canonical regions provide a concrete specification for software that evaluates the optimal boundary area at a prescribed volume in a cubic periodic domain. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The classification concerns global static perimeter minimizers. It supplies no interface evolution, collision response, or relaxation dynamics for a simulation. |
| tools | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The outward arithmetic prescription constructs a deterministic verification tool: integer endpoint operations enclose the scalar expressions, and rational comparisons certify the interpolation margins. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The capped scalar gain has an explicit evaluation formula. It replaces the defining infimum by a critical-point candidate and a capped candidate, with a proof covering feasibility changes and boundary cases. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The coordinate down-set describes geometric occupancy and constrains possible vertex patterns. The paper does not construct a discrete representation with insertion, deletion, or query operations. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The arithmetic prescription specifies enclosing integer operations, but provides no accelerator architecture, scheduling construction, or hardware performance analysis. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Reflection invariance is a geometric property of minimizing regions. It establishes no cryptographic primitive, adversarial security model, or security reduction. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The curvature estimate concerns a scalar gain in the geometric proof. It provides no learning procedure, statistical model, or generalization guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | For the stated periodic perimeter problem, the explicit branches and region formulas construct an exact optimal-value reference and canonical optimal shapes. These can validate a geometric optimizer under the same volume and metric constraints. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The flux and coarea identities relate planar slices of a continuous surface. They do not construct a communication protocol or establish consistency, fault tolerance, or distributed termination. |
| other (Geometric variational benchmarks) | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The theorem explicitly constructs balls, circular tubes, coordinate slabs, and their complements as canonical minimizers. Their stated dimensions and transition ties provide geometric benchmark families for the cubic periodic isoperimetric problem. |

          "anchor": "num:arcsine-enclosure",
          "exact_quote": "For a precise common rounding rule, take $N=10^{40}$ and represent an interval by integer endpoints $[a,b]_N=[a/N,b/N]$. Enclose each rational input $r$ by $[\\lfloor Nr\\rfloor,\\lceil Nr\\rceil]_N$. Addition and negation are exact at this scale. For multiplication use $$[a,b]_N[c,d]_N\\subseteq\n \\left[\\left\\lfloor\\frac{\\min\\{ac,ad,bc,bd\\}}N\\right\\rfloor,\n       \\left\\lceil\\frac{\\max\\{ac,ad,bc,bd\\}}N\\right\\rceil\\right]_N.$$ For $0<a\\le b$, reciprocal is enclosed by $[\\lfloor N^2/b\\rfloor,\\lceil N^2/a\\rceil]_N$; negative intervals are treated by negation. Division is multiplication by this reciprocal. For $0\\le a\\le b$, square root is enclosed by $$[\\lfloor\\sqrt{aN}\\rfloor,\\lceil\\sqrt{bN}\\rceil]_N,$$ whose endpoints are found by integer-square comparisons. The zeroth power is $[N,N]_N$. An odd nonnegative integer power takes its extrema at the endpoints; an even positive power has lower endpoint zero if the interval contains zero, and otherwise the smaller endpoint power. Divide those integer powers by $N^{n-1}$ and round outward. For minimum and maximum, apply the same operation separately to the two lower and the two upper endpoints. Each rule encloses every possible value of its operation, also when its inputs are dependent. Induction therefore proves the enclosure property for any expression formed by these rules.",

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-cubic-ambient-metric** (formal_scope): The perimeter profile and complete equality classification concern the unit cubic flat torus. An extension to other periodic lattices is absent. Blocked use: ['Applying the same phase thresholds or canonical minimizer classification to a non-cubic periodic metric.']
- **limit-fixed-volume-perimeter** (hypothesis): The objective is ordinary perimeter among finite-perimeter competitors with prescribed volume. Blocked use: ['Replacing perimeter by an anisotropic or spatially weighted interface energy.', 'Using the result as an unconstrained interface minimization theorem.']
- **limit-normalization-and-curvature** (formal_scope): The geometric argument uses the length-doubled torus and normalized area. Its curvature is the outward sum of principal curvatures in that metric. Blocked use: ['Comparing normalized area directly with unit-torus perimeter.', 'Rescaling curvature as though it were normalized area.']
- **limit-global-minimizers-versus-stable-surfaces** (hypothesis): The structural classification assumes an isoperimetric region with closed boundary in a complete orientable flat manifold. It does not classify every stable constant mean curvature surface. Blocked use: ['Concluding that an arbitrary stable surface is one of the global minimizing regions.']
- **limit-transition-cominimizers** (hypothesis): The reduction requires every minimizer at both transition volumes to be standard. Endpoint profile values alone do not supply the complete equality classification. Blocked use: ['Using equality of endpoint optimal values as a substitute for excluding additional endpoint minimizers.']
- **limit-reflection-and-downset-stage** (hypothesis): The reflection and strict coordinate monotonicity arguments concern a nonstandard minimizing boundary, with connectedness, analyticity and higher genus available. Blocked use: ['Assuming that an arbitrary periodic region can be translated into a reflection-invariant down-set without changing the problem.']
- **limit-three-vertex-side-choice** (edge_case): The vertex-pattern reduction initially permits the smaller-volume-side choice only for the four-vertex pattern. The corresponding choice for the three-vertex pattern requires the later flux argument. Blocked use: ['Assigning the three-vertex occupied pattern to the smaller-volume side solely from the vertex count.']
- **limit-regular-slicing-and-substitution** (hypothesis): The section identities use the reflected coordinate-monotone geometry. Exceptional heights are handled through absolute continuity and area-coordinate substitution rather than by assuming every slice is regular. Blocked use: ['Applying the regular-level formulas pointwise at a critical height without justification.', 'Assuming a smooth inverse for the section-area function.']
- **limit-nesting-configuration** (hypothesis): The nesting improvement assumes the three-vertex configuration at the cylinder–slab transition, the competitor area bound, and slicing along the missing axis with empty top sections. Blocked use: ['Applying the improved area defect to arbitrary nested planar families or other transition configurations.']
- **limit-bottom-containment-both-orientations** (hypothesis): The strip endpoint restriction comes from containment in the bottom slice and calibration of both complementary-corner extents. Equal extents and convexity are not assumptions of the argument. Blocked use: ['Transferring the strip perimeter gain to sections that are not contained in the calibrated bottom slice.', 'Replacing both extent estimates by an unsupported symmetry assumption.']
- **limit-central-gain-and-zero-width** (edge_case): The shape gain is restricted to its stated central curvature interval. Its normalized slice coordinate is introduced only when the gain interval has positive width. Blocked use: ['Extending the central gain estimate beyond its proved curvature range.', 'Dividing by a vanishing gain-interval width.']
- **limit-baseline-insufficient-in-central-range** (counterexample_scope): The baseline alone is insufficient in the central curvature range; the nested-section gain is needed to establish the strict area contradiction there. Blocked use: ['Claiming positivity throughout the curvature window from the planar perimeter envelope alone.']
- **limit-taylor-remainder-correction** (approximation): The approximate baseline must be corrected by the stated one-sided Taylor remainder before it is used as a lower bound for the exact baseline. Blocked use: ['Treating the cubic Taylor evaluation as an exact baseline value.', 'Dropping the correction when certifying a knot or interpolation margin.']
- **limit-outward-arithmetic-and-branch-selection** (approximation): The scalar knot certificates require enclosing arithmetic, the arcsine-series tail, and justified branch selection. Ordinary approximate evaluation does not establish the displayed rational inequalities. Blocked use: ['Certifying strict positivity from floating-point agreement alone.', 'Choosing a Taylor branch when the comparison enclosures overlap.', 'Discarding series tails or inward-rounding intermediate operations.']
- **limit-interpolation-analytic-premises** (hypothesis): Positive knot bounds extend to the full intervals only through the proved upper second derivative bounds and the prescribed interpolation loss. Blocked use: ['Inferring continuous positivity from sampled knot values alone.', 'Using an interpolation coefficient outside its justified interval.']
- **limit-capped-gain-feasibility** (edge_case): The scalar gain formula compares the cap value with the uncapped critical-point value and accounts for cases where a displayed candidate is infeasible. Blocked use: ['Returning the uncapped critical-point value without comparing the cap.', 'Changing the objective or cap rule while retaining the same evaluation formula.']
- **limit-finite-arithmetic-versus-geometric-proof** (nonconstructive): Finite rational interpolation comparisons certify only the arithmetic part of the argument. They do not independently establish compactness, analytic continuation, stability, section identities, or the geometric classification. Blocked use: ['Presenting successful rational gap checks as an independent certificate for the entire isoperimetric theorem.', 'Claiming a general mesh optimization procedure from the classification proof.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 354 (`lean/docs/354.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The Isoperimetric Conjecture for the Cubic Flat Three-Torus](../../preprints/The-Isoperimetric-Conjecture-for-the-Cubic-Flat-Three-Torus-September-24-2026/article.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization determines the isoperimetric profile and all minimizers in the unit cubic flat three-torus. At every volume $0<V<1$, a minimizer exists, and the minimizers, up to null-set changes, are exactly balls, circular tubes about shortest closed geodesics, coordinate slabs, and their complements in the appropriate volume ranges.
>
> The classification includes all equality cases at the transition volumes $4\pi/81$ and $1/\pi$, and every minimizer has the stated candidate-profile perimeter.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open MeasureTheory Set
open scoped ENNReal

namespace CubicTorus

abbrev Cover := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 3)
abbrev Torus := PiLp 2 (fun _ : Fin 3 => AddCircle (1 : ℝ))

/-- The quotient R³ → R³/Z³ in orthonormal coordinates. -/
def quotientMap (x : Cover) : Torus :=
  WithLp.toLp 2 (fun i => (x i : AddCircle (1 : ℝ)))

/-- One unit-volume fundamental domain; its boundary is immaterial to integration. -/
def fundamentalCube : Set Cover := {x | ∀ i, 0 ≤ x i ∧ x i < 1}

/-- The Riemannian volume measure of the unit flat torus. -/
def torusVolume : Measure Torus :=
  Measure.map quotientMap (volume.restrict fundamentalCube)

/-- An integer lattice vector in the Euclidean cover. -/
def latticeVector (n : Fin 3 → ℤ) : Cover := WithLp.toLp 2 (fun i => (n i : ℝ))

/-- The smooth vector fields on the flat torus with pointwise length at most one,
expressed as Z³-periodic fields on its Euclidean cover. The explicit ℕ∞ index
is C^∞; top of WithTop ℕ∞ would instead mean analytic in this Mathlib. -/
def AdmissibleField (F : Cover → Cover) : Prop :=
  ContDiff ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) F ∧
  (∀ (n : Fin 3 → ℤ) (x : Cover), F (x + latticeVector n) = F x) ∧
  ∀ x, ‖F x‖ ≤ 1

/-- Euclidean divergence in the orthonormal coordinate frame. -/
def divergence (F : Cover → Cover) (x : Cover) : ℝ :=
  ∑ i : Fin 3, (fderiv ℝ F x (EuclideanSpace.single i 1)) i

/-- De Giorgi perimeter, allowed to be infinite; no regularity of E is imposed. -/
def perimeter (E : Set Torus) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  ⨆ (F : Cover → Cover) (_ : AdmissibleField F),
    ENNReal.ofReal (∫ x in fundamentalCube ∩ quotientMap ⁻¹' E, divergence F x)

/-- Finite-perimeter measurable regions, including all null-set modifications. -/
def FinitePerimeter (E : Set Torus) : Prop :=
  NullMeasurableSet E torusVolume ∧ perimeter E < ⊤

/-- A global perimeter minimizer under the prescribed volume constraint. -/
def IsMinimizerAt (V : ℝ) (E : Set Torus) : Prop :=
  FinitePerimeter E ∧ torusVolume E = ENNReal.ofReal V ∧
  ∀ F : Set Torus, FinitePerimeter F → torusVolume F = ENNReal.ofReal V →
    perimeter E ≤ perimeter F

/-- The candidate unit-torus isoperimetric profile. -/
def candidateProfile (V : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  let v := min V (1 - V)
  min ((36 * Real.pi) ^ (1 / 3 : ℝ) * v ^ (2 / 3 : ℝ))
    (min (2 * Real.sqrt (Real.pi * v)) 2)

/-- Canonical centered ball. Its radius on the listed branch is below 1/2. -/
def canonicalBall (V : ℝ) : Set Torus :=
  Metric.ball 0 ((3 * V / (4 * Real.pi)) ^ (1 / 3 : ℝ))

/-- Canonical tube around the shortest closed geodesic in coordinate direction 0. -/
def canonicalTube (V : ℝ) : Set Torus :=
  {x | ‖x 1‖ ^ 2 + ‖x 2‖ ^ 2 < (Real.sqrt (V / Real.pi)) ^ 2}

/-- Canonical slab of width V between two parallel coordinate tori. -/
def canonicalSlab (V : ℝ) : Set Torus := {x | ‖x 0‖ < V / 2}

/-- Equality of regions up to ambient flat isometry and a null set. -/
def CongruentAE (E F : Set Torus) : Prop :=
  ∃ φ : Torus ≃ᵢ Torus, E =ᵐ[torusVolume] (φ '' F)

/-- Exactly the closed phase ranges, so adjacent types are BOTH included at ties. -/
def SmallStandardRegion (V : ℝ) (E : Set Torus) : Prop :=
  (V ≤ 4 * Real.pi / 81 ∧ CongruentAE E (canonicalBall V)) ∨
  (4 * Real.pi / 81 ≤ V ∧ V ≤ 1 / Real.pi ∧ CongruentAE E (canonicalTube V)) ∨
  (1 / Real.pi ≤ V ∧ CongruentAE E (canonicalSlab V))

/-- The equality class at every interior volume, including complementation. -/
def StandardRegionAt (V : ℝ) (E : Set Torus) : Prop :=
  if V ≤ 1 / 2 then SmallStandardRegion V E
  else SmallStandardRegion (1 - V) Eᶜ

/-- Attainment, the sharp profile, and all equality cases at interior volumes. -/
theorem unit_cubic_isoperimetric (V : ℝ) (hV : V ∈ Ioo 0 1) :
    (∃ E : Set Torus, IsMinimizerAt V E) ∧
    (∀ E : Set Torus, IsMinimizerAt V E →
      perimeter E = ENNReal.ofReal (candidateProfile V)) ∧
    (∀ E : Set Torus, IsMinimizerAt V E ↔ StandardRegionAt V E) := by
  sorry

end CubicTorus
end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the isoperimetric conjecture for the cubic flat three-torus and classify all minimizers, including the equality cases at the transition volumes $`4\pi/81`$ and $`1/\pi`$. The minimizing regions are balls, circular tubes about shortest closed geodesics, coordinate slabs, and their complements.

<a id="intro:section"></a>
### Introduction

The isoperimetric conjecture for the cubic flat three-torus predicts a particularly simple sequence of minimizing regions: balls, circular tubes about shortest closed geodesics, and slabs between parallel coordinate tori, followed by their complements. It is a concrete instance of the periodic isoperimetric problem, in which the local Euclidean geometry coexists with global topological constraints. We resolve this conjecture and classify every minimizing region, including all equality cases at the two transition volumes.

<a id="stmt-intro-main"></a>
<div id="intro:main" class="theorem">

<a id="intro:profile"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *For $V\in(0,1)$, put $v=\min(V,1-V)$. Among finite-perimeter subsets of $\mathbb R^3/\mathbb Z^3$ of volume $V$, the minimum perimeter is $$I_{\rm unit}(V)=
 \min\left\{(36\pi)^{1/3}v^{2/3},\ 2\sqrt{\pi v},\ 2\right\}.
 \label{intro:profile}$$ For $0<V\le1/2$, every minimizing region is, up to an ambient isometry and a null set, one of the following: $$\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{volume range}&\text{region}\\ \hline
0<V\le 4\pi/81 & \text{a ball of radius }(3V/(4\pi))^{1/3}\\[2pt]
4\pi/81\le V\le1/\pi
 &\text{a solid circular tube of radius }\sqrt{V/\pi}\\
 &\text{about a shortest closed geodesic}\\[2pt]
1/\pi\le V\le1/2
 &\text{a slab of width }V\text{ between coordinate tori}.
\end{array}$$ Both listed types occur at each common endpoint of adjacent ranges, and there are no further equality cases. For $V>1/2$, the minimizing regions are the complements of those at volume $1-V$. At zero or full volume the conclusion is trivial.*

</div>

The ball–cylinder–slab prediction appears explicitly in the periodic isoperimetric problem of Hauswirth, Pérez, Romon, and Ros <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[HPRR](#ref-HPRR), Introduction] and in Ros’s account of the cubic torus and cube <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Ros](#ref-Ros), Section 1.6]. Reflection relates the periodic problem to relative perimeter in a cube: the canonical regions become an eighth ball about a vertex, a quarter cylinder about an edge, and a region cut off by a coordinate plane. The remaining geometric difficulty is to exclude connected boundaries of higher genus.

Several parts of the predicted profile were known. Hadwiger proved minimality of a coordinate cut at half volume for the cube <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Hadwiger](#ref-Hadwiger)]; Barthe and Maurey recovered this result by Gaussian comparison <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[BartheMaurey](#ref-BartheMaurey), Theorem 7 and the following discussion]. Morgan and Johnson’s sharp small-volume comparison implies that balls minimize on a compact flat manifold for sufficiently small volumes <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[MorganJohnson](#ref-MorganJohnson), Theorem 4.4]. Acerbi, Fusco, and Morini proved that coordinate slabs are the only minimizers near half volume in the cubic three-torus, up to translations and coordinate permutations <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[AcerbiFuscoMorini](#ref-AcerbiFuscoMorini), Theorem 5.3]. These small-volume and near-half-volume neighborhoods were obtained without effective bounds reaching the proposed transitions.

The geometric tools also have a substantial history. Coordinate reflection symmetry for orthogonal translation lattices is attributed to Hsiang in Ritoré’s thesis <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[RitoreThesis](#ref-RitoreThesis), Theorem 1.19]. Ritoré and Ros developed the low-genus stability and structure results for flat three-manifolds <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[RitoreRos92](#ref-RitoreRos92), [RitoreRos96](#ref-RitoreRos96)]. Hauswirth, Pérez, Romon, and Ros established the connectedness, genus and curvature–area estimates used here and the differential comparison method for the isoperimetric profile <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[HPRR](#ref-HPRR)]. The coordinate graph property after reflection is Ritoré’s observation, recorded with its stability proof by Ros <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Ros](#ref-Ros), Proposition 2.3].

Milman obtained effective ranges for the canonical phases, including the cubic spherical range up to volume fraction $0.120582\ldots$ <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Milman](#ref-Milman), Theorem 1.2]. He also made explicit the reduction of the profile conjecture to $4\pi/81$ and $1/\pi$, using the method of Hauswirth, Pérez, Romon, and Ros <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Milman](#ref-Milman), Theorem 1.3 and Section 4.3]. This reduction classifies all minimizers inside the smooth branches once the endpoint profile values are known; additional co-minimizers at the transitions still have to be excluded. We establish those endpoint exclusions directly.

The main new ingredient is a quantitative use of nested planar sections at the cylinder–slab transition. Milman’s base-profile comparison identifies the lack of nesting among planar minimizers as an obstruction to sharpness <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Milman](#ref-Milman), Corollary 2.8 and the surrounding discussion]. Here the planar isoperimetric inequality alone leaves a deficit in the central curvature range. A calibration of the complementary corner in the bottom section controls both edge extents; nesting then forces a definite length excess in intermediate strip sections. A Jensen estimate and the section flux identity turn that excess into a strict bound for the total area. The other configurations are excluded by scalar inequalities, and explicit outward arithmetic bounds complete the remaining one-variable estimate.

Section <a href="#pre:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:section">2</a> reduces the classification to $V_1=4\pi/81$ and $V_2=1/\pi$. Reflection and volume-constrained stability place any nonstandard minimizer in a coordinate-monotone position in a unit cube, with one of the two vertex patterns of Section <a href="#geo:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:section">3</a>. Section <a href="#slice:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="slice:section">4</a> derives the flux, projection, and coarea identities for planar sections. Section <a href="#slice:early-exclusions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="slice:early-exclusions">5</a> uses them to exclude the four-vertex pattern at both volumes and the three-vertex pattern at $V_1$. Section <a href="#nest:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:section">6</a> improves the planar lower bound for the remaining three-vertex pattern at $V_2$. Section <a href="#completion:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="completion:section">7</a> assembles the endpoint exclusions with the two-volume reduction and restores the unit-torus normalization. Appendix <a href="#num:elementary-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:elementary-estimates">8</a> proves the scalar inequality used to exclude the four-vertex pattern, and Appendix <a href="#num:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:section">9</a> certifies the remaining one-variable bounds. The reproducible exact calculations are included with the source.

<a id="pre:section"></a>
### Classical inputs and the two-volume reduction

We work on $$T=\mathbb R^3/(2\mathbb Z^3),
 \qquad V(E)=\frac{|E|}{8},
 \qquad A(E)=\frac{|\partial E|}{8}.$$ Here and below perimeter is understood in the finite-perimeter sense; a minimizer is represented by its regular region. The map from the unit torus to $T$ doubles lengths. Consequently $V$ is the original volume fraction, whereas $A$ is one half of the original perimeter. The outward unit normal is $N$, and $h=k_1+k_2$ is the sum of the principal curvatures in the metric of $T$, positive on a ball. It is not divided by eight.

Standard compactness and regularity for isoperimetric regions imply existence at every $0<V<1$, with smooth embedded boundary of constant mean curvature. In a flat metric this boundary is real analytic: its local CMC graph equation is analytic elliptic, so <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Blatt](#ref-Blatt), Theorem 1.1] applies. The second variation is nonnegative on all volume-preserving variations. We use the following classical structural theorem in precisely its unrestricted, identity-group case.

<a id="stmt-pre-structure"></a>
<div id="pre:structure" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Classical flat-manifold structure). *Let $E$ be an isoperimetric region in a complete orientable flat three-manifold, with closed boundary $\Sigma$. Either $\Sigma$ is a union of parallel planar surfaces, or $\Sigma$ is connected. In the connected case:*

1.  *genus zero gives a round sphere;*

2.  *genus one gives a flat quotient of a plane or a circular cylinder;*

3.  *the genus is at most four;*

4.  *in genus two or three, $H^2|\Sigma|\le 2\pi$, where $H=h/2$; in genus four, $H=0$.*

</div>

<a id="pre:curvature"></a>
These statements are Theorems 5 and 7 of Hauswirth–Pérez–Romon–Ros <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[HPRR](#ref-HPRR)], applied with the identity symmetry group. In particular the orientation-preserving hypothesis of their Theorem 7 is satisfied. Their genus-one assertion builds on the stability results of Ritoré–Ros <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[RitoreRos92](#ref-RitoreRos92), [RitoreRos96](#ref-RitoreRos96)]. Existence and regularity in the compact torus setting are recorded in <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[HPRR](#ref-HPRR), Theorem 3]. We call a minimizing boundary *standard* if it is planar, spherical, or a circular-cylinder quotient. Every nonstandard minimizing boundary is therefore connected of genus $g\ge2$ and satisfies $$h^2 A\le\pi.
 \label{pre:curvature}$$ Indeed $h^2A=H^2|\Sigma|/2$, and the genus-four case has $h=0$.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The standard competitors

<a id="pre:envelope"></a>
For $0\le V\le1$, set $v=\min(V,1-V)$ and $$F(V)=\min\left\{
       (9\pi/2)^{1/3}v^{2/3},\ \sqrt{\pi v},\ 1
       \right\},
 \qquad V_1=\frac{4\pi}{81},\quad V_2=\frac1\pi.
 \label{pre:envelope}$$ The first, second, and third expressions are the respective minima on $0\le v\le V_1$, $V_1\le v\le V_2$, and $V_2\le v\le1/2$.

<a id="stmt-pre-standard"></a>
<div id="pre:standard" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *The function $F$ is attained by standard competitors. Every standard minimizing boundary has $A\ge F(V)$; equality gives exactly the balls, shortest-geodesic tubes, and coordinate slabs in Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>, including the indicated ties and complements.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-standard"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-standard</summary>

*Proof.* A sphere in $T$ lifts to an embedded sphere in $\mathbb R^3$. Its distinct lattice translates have disjoint interiors: equal-radius spheres with intersecting interiors would have intersecting boundaries. Thus it bounds an embedded ball on one side. For its radius $R$, $$W=\frac{\pi R^3}{6},\qquad A=\frac{\pi R^2}{2}
       =(9\pi/2)^{1/3}W^{2/3},$$ where $W$ denotes the ball-side volume. Since $W\ge v$, this area is at least the first expression in <a href="#pre:envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:envelope">[pre:envelope]</a>.

For a compact circular-cylinder quotient, the stabilizer of its lifted axis contains a nonzero axial lattice translation. Its primitive core therefore has length $2k$, with $k=|m|\ge1$ for a primitive nonzero $m\in\mathbb Z^3$. Embeddedness gives a solid tube on one side, with $$W=\frac{k\pi R^2}{4},\qquad A=\frac{k\pi R}{2}
       =\sqrt{\pi kW}\ge\sqrt{\pi v}.$$ This comparison concerns the area of the given tube and does not require a shortest tube of the possibly larger volume $W$ to embed. If such a boundary minimizes, $A=I(V)\le F(V)$, where $I$ is the isoperimetric profile. Equality throughout forces $k=1$, $W=v$, and $v$ in the closed cylindrical branch. The corresponding sphere comparison similarly forces $W=v$ and the spherical branch.

A connected closed planar surface has a primitive integral normal $m$ and physical area $4|m|$. Disjoint planar surfaces must be parallel, and a single such torus does not separate $T$. At least two are required to bound a region. Their total normalized area is therefore at least one, with equality only for two tori with $|m|=1$. Their intervening regions are exactly coordinate slabs.

Finally the branches in <a href="#pre:envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:envelope">[pre:envelope]</a> are realized by an embedded ball, a shortest tube, and a coordinate slab, respectively. At the last volume of its branch the ball radius is $2/3$; the largest tube radius in its branch is $2/\pi$. Both are less than the transverse injectivity radius one. The branch intersections are exactly $V_1,V_2$. Complements give the remaining volumes. This proves both attainment and the asserted equality classification. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Upper supports for the profile

<a id="stmt-pre-supports"></a>
<div id="pre:supports" class="lemma">

<a id="pre:parallel"></a>
**Lemma 4**. *The profile $I:[0,1]\to[0,\infty)$ is continuous, concave, and satisfies $I(V)=I(1-V)$. If a minimizer at $V\in(0,1)$ has sum curvature $h$, there is a smooth local upper support $C$ for $I$ at $V$ such that $C'(V)=h$ and $C''(V)\le0$. If its boundary is connected of genus $g$, then at contact $$C^2C''=-h^2C+\pi(1-g).
 \label{pre:parallel}$$ Every such contact curvature is nonnegative for $V<1/2$ and nonpositive for $V>1/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-supports"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-supports</summary>

*Proof.* Compactness of finite-perimeter sets gives lower semicontinuity of $I$. A local volume-changing smooth variation of a minimizer gives upper semicontinuity at every interior volume. Since $0\le I\le F$, continuity also holds at the endpoints. Complementation gives the symmetry.

Let $K=k_1k_2$ be the intrinsic Gauss curvature of $\Sigma$. Let $a(\tau)$ and $w(\tau)$ be normalized area and volume under outward normal parallel displacement by $\tau$. At $\tau=0$, $$w'=A,\qquad a'=hA,\qquad w''=hA,\qquad
 a''=\frac18\int_\Sigma 2K\,dS.$$ Small parallels are embedded and $w'>0$; expressing their area as a function $C(w)$ provides the upper support. The chain rule gives $$C'=h,\qquad
 C^2C''=\frac18\int_\Sigma 2K\,dS-h^2A.$$ For connected $\Sigma$, Gauss–Bonnet gives <a href="#pre:parallel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:parallel">[pre:parallel]</a>. For an arbitrary boundary, $$\frac18\int_\Sigma(2K-h^2)\,dS
       =-\frac18\int_\Sigma(k_1^2+k_2^2)\,dS\le0,$$ so $C''\le0$. These are the parallel-variation formulas of <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[HPRR](#ref-HPRR), Section 3], with the stated normalization.

For completeness, this contact property implies concavity without differentiability of $I$. Suppose $I(c)<L(c)$ for a chord $L$ on $[a,b]$ and $a<c<b$. Choose $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small that $I(c)<L(c)+\varepsilon(c-a)(c-b)$. The minimum of $$I(x)-L(x)-\varepsilon(x-a)(x-b)$$ is negative and occurs at an interior point $x_0$. Adding this minimum to the strictly convex quadratic produces a function $q\le I$ with equality at $x_0$. Any local upper support there satisfies $C''(x_0)\ge q''=2\varepsilon$, a contradiction.

For a concave function, the slope of a smooth upper support lies between its one-sided derivatives: $I'_+(V)\le h\le I'_-(V)$. The secant joining the equal values at $V$ and $1-V$ has slope zero. Concavity therefore gives the asserted signs on either side of $1/2$. No curvature conclusion at exactly $1/2$ is needed at this stage. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pre-reduction"></a>
<div id="pre:reduction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (Two-volume reduction). *If every minimizer at $V_1$ and $V_2$ is standard, then $I=F$ on $[0,1]$, and every minimizer is one of the standard regions of Lemma <a href="#pre:standard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:standard">3</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* The assumption, existence, and Lemma <a href="#pre:standard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:standard">3</a> give $I=F$ at $V_1,V_2$, and hence also at $1-V_2,1-V_1$. On each open interval between consecutive points in $$0,\ V_1,\ V_2,\ 1-V_2,\ 1-V_1,\ 1,$$ the function $F$ is smooth and <a id="pre:branch-ode"></a>$$F''+\frac{(F')^2}{F}\ge0.
 \label{pre:branch-ode}$$ This follows directly for its power-law and constant branches.

If $I-F$ had a negative minimum on one of the closed intervals, it would occur in its interior. A minimizer there must be nonstandard by Lemma <a href="#pre:standard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:standard">3</a>. For its upper support, $C=I<F$, $C'=F'$, and $C''\ge F''$ at contact. Hence $$C''+\frac{(C')^2}{C}
       \ge F''+\frac{(F')^2}{F}\ge0,$$ whereas <a href="#pre:parallel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:parallel">[pre:parallel]</a> makes the left-hand side $\pi(1-g)/C^2<0$. Thus $I=F$ throughout.

Now consider any nonstandard minimizer at an interior point of one of the smooth intervals. Its support touches $F=I$ from above, so $C'=F'$ and $C''\ge F''$, giving the same contradiction with $C=F$. The partition points are covered by the hypothesis and complementation. In particular $1/2$ belongs to the smooth constant interval, so it is included. Lemma <a href="#pre:standard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:standard">3</a> completes the equality classification. ◻

</details>

The profile comparison and the classification inside each smooth branch are Milman’s Theorem 1.3 and Propositions 4.4–4.6 , based on the differential method of Hauswirth–Pérez–Romon–Ros. The endpoint hypothesis here additionally excludes nonstandard co-minimizers at the transitions. Establishing that hypothesis is the task of the remaining geometric argument.

<a id="geo:section"></a>
### Reflection symmetry and the two vertex patterns

Throughout this section $\Sigma=\partial E$ is a nonstandard minimizing boundary in $T$. In particular it is connected, nonplanar, real analytic, and of genus at least two. We first prove that the original region has coordinate reflection symmetry, then use stability to restrict its possible vertex patterns.

<a id="stmt-geo-reflection"></a>
<div id="geo:reflection" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *After translating the coordinate origins, the original region $E$ is invariant under reflection in each of the six coordinate tori $x_i=0,1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-reflection"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-reflection</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a coordinate. Let $H_a$ be the half-torus between $x_i=a$ and $x_i=a+1$. The occupied volume in $H_a$ is continuous in $a$, and its value at $a+1$ is the complementary half of the total occupied volume. Thus some $H_a$ bisects the occupied volume.

Reflecting each half separately across its two end tori gives two competitors of the original volume. Their characteristic-function traces agree across each reflecting seam, so the reflection adds no seam perimeter, even if the cutting torus is tangent to the original surface. The intersection of the nonplanar analytic surface with either cutting torus has zero surface area: positive area would force an analytic open planar piece and hence a planar connected surface. The average perimeter of the two competitors is therefore the original minimum. Each competitor is minimizing and consequently has smooth analytic boundary.

Each open half has both positive occupied volume and positive vacant volume, so it contains an open patch of the original boundary. A reflected competitor shares such a patch. This patch cannot be planar, and Theorem <a href="#pre:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:structure">2</a> makes the competitor’s boundary connected. Two connected closed embedded analytic surfaces sharing an open patch coincide everywhere. To see that local coincidence is closed, at a limit point choose analytic graph charts over their common limiting tangent plane. Their graph functions agree on a nonempty open set, and hence on the connected chart by the identity theorem. Connectedness then propagates equality across the whole surface.

The occupied sides also agree. Their outward normals agree on the unchanged patch; the relative sign of the normal fields is constant on the connected common boundary. Equivalently, equality of their oriented boundary distributions makes the difference of the characteristic functions constant on the connected torus, and agreement on the retained half makes that constant zero. This reasoning does not require $h\ne0$. The original region, not merely a chosen competitor, is therefore reflection invariant. Repeat in the other two coordinates. Translations of their origins preserve the symmetries already obtained. ◻

</details>

We may now work in $Q=[0,1]^3$. Normalized area and volume in $T$ are exactly relative perimeter and ordinary volume in $Q$. The outward normal transforms equivariantly under each reflection. In particular $N_i=0$ on the face $x_i=0$ or $1$: the surface meets a face orthogonally.

<a id="stmt-geo-normals"></a>
<div id="geo:normals" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:strict-normal"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Strict coordinate monotonicity). *After possibly reversing individual coordinates in $Q$, $$N_i>0\qquad\text{on }\Sigma\text{ whenever }0<x_i<1.
 \label{geo:strict-normal}$$ Consequently $E\cap Q$ is a down-set for the coordinatewise order.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-normals"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-normals</summary>

*Proof.* On the entire torus surface, $u=N_i$ satisfies the Jacobi equation $$Lu=0,\qquad L=\Delta_\Sigma+|\mathrm{II}|^2,$$ because axial translations preserve mean curvature. It is odd under the corresponding reflection. If it vanished identically, the complete axial circle field would be tangent to $\Sigma$; its flow would preserve $\Sigma$. The quotient by this free circle action is a closed connected curve, making $\Sigma$ a torus, contrary to its genus.

We spell out the constrained nodal-domain argument. The stability form is $$\mathcal Q(f,f)=\int_\Sigma
       \bigl(|\nabla f|^2-|\mathrm{II}|^2f^2\bigr)\,dS\ge0
       \quad\text{if }\int_\Sigma f\,dS=0.$$ The restriction $u_D$ to a nodal domain $D$, extended by zero, belongs to $H^1(\Sigma)$ and satisfies $\mathcal Q(u_D,u_D)=0$. This follows by testing $Lu=0$ with the restriction, or first truncating the signed values at distance $\varepsilon$ from zero and passing to the limit. Restrictions to distinct nodal domains are orthogonal for $\mathcal Q$. If there were at least three nodal domains, a nonzero combination $f$ of two restrictions could be chosen with zero mean. It would have $\mathcal Q(f,f)=0$. Polarizing the stability inequality against all mean-zero tests gives $Lf=c$ weakly for a constant $c$. On the unused nodal domain $f=0$, so $c=0$. Local elliptic regularity first bootstraps this weak solution to a smooth one. The Jacobi coefficients are analytic because $\Sigma$ is, so analytic elliptic regularity <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Blatt](#ref-Blatt), Theorem 1.1] and the identity theorem force $f=0$ everywhere, a contradiction. Thus $u$ has at most two nodal domains.

It vanishes on the two reflection tori. An additional zero in either open half would force both signs locally: a one-signed Jacobi solution cannot have an interior zero by the strong minimum principle and unique continuation. Reflection would give both signs in the other half, hence at least four nodal domains. Different signs on different pieces of a half give the same contradiction. Therefore the sign is strict and fixed on each half. Reverse coordinates to obtain <a href="#geo:strict-normal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:strict-normal">[geo:strict-normal]</a>.

An increasing coordinate segment meets $\Sigma$, when it meets it in its interior, transversely and only from the occupied to the vacant side. It cannot cross twice. Following coordinate segments proves the down-set assertion, also on the faces by continuity. ◻

</details>

This is the graph observation of Ritoré, presented with its stability proof in Ros <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Ros](#ref-Ros), Proposition 2.3]. The explicit argument above will also control the exceptional slicing levels.

<a id="stmt-geo-patterns"></a>
<div id="geo:patterns" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:two-patterns"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Vertex patterns). *No vertex of $Q$ lies on $\Sigma$. Up to taking the complementary region and reversing all coordinates, and up to coordinate permutations, the only possible vertex sets for a nonstandard minimizer are $$\{000,100,010\},\qquad
 \{000,100,010,001\}.
 \label{geo:two-patterns}$$ The four-vertex pattern may be placed on the smaller-volume side. No such assertion is yet made for the three-vertex pattern.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-patterns"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-patterns</summary>

*Proof.* At a vertex, equivariance under all three reflections would force all components of a boundary normal to vanish. Thus each vertex lies strictly on one side. The occupied vertices form a nonempty proper order ideal, containing $000$ and excluding $111$. Complementation followed by $x\mapsto(1,1,1)-x$ preserves the down-set property and replaces the number of occupied vertices by its complement to eight. We may therefore assume at most four occupied vertices.

For one occupied vertex, the three opposite faces are strictly vacant. Indeed a point on such a face in the closure of the region would, by monotonicity, put its nearest axial vertex in the closure as well. Compactness gives a gap from those faces. Reflect about the occupied corner to lift the region into a box centered there. Every radial ray starts inside and crosses the boundary exactly once, transversely: at a boundary point $x\ne0$, $\sum_i x_iN_i>0$, with signs interpreted by reflection. The radial function is smooth by the implicit function theorem. The boundary is a sphere.

For two occupied vertices, they form an edge, say in the $x$-direction. The entire corresponding axial circle is occupied, with a neighborhood, and the other two opposite faces are separated from the region by gaps. In the cover $S^1_x\times\mathbb R^2$, radial rays in the transverse plane about this circle cross once and transversely, since $yN_y+zN_z>0$ at the boundary. A smooth radial function over $S^1\times S^1$ parametrizes the boundary, which has genus one. Both cases are excluded.

Three occupied vertices necessarily give the first pattern in <a href="#geo:two-patterns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:two-patterns">[geo:two-patterns]</a>. Four occupied vertices either give the second pattern or form an entire face. In the latter case that whole face is strictly occupied and the opposite face strictly vacant. The strict normal signs give a graph between them, separated from both end faces. Reflection produces two disjoint graphs over the transverse torus, contradicting connectedness of $\Sigma$.

Finally the four-vertex pattern is carried to itself by complementation and reversal of all coordinates. It may thus be assigned to the smaller-volume side. Reducing the vertex count alone would not establish the analogous assertion for the three-vertex pattern; its occupied-side choice will follow from the flux identity. ◻

</details>

Visual witness description of PDF page 8 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):
