<a id="slice:section"></a>
### Identities for planar sections

Coordinate monotonicity lets us describe the boundary through planar sections. The identities below convert their perimeters and area changes into bounds on the total boundary area at the two transition volumes.

Choose an axial direction $z$ in the reflected cube $Q$, and write $$E_z=\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^2:(x,y,z)\in E\},\qquad
 v(z)=|E_z|,\quad s=v(0),\quad r=v(1).$$ Let $p(z)$ be the relative perimeter of $E_z$ in the square. In slice integrals, $\partial E_z$ denotes the separating curve $\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^2:(x,y,z)\in\Sigma\}$, including its endpoints; portions of the square boundary are not counted. The surface coarea Jacobian for the height function is $$\alpha=|\nabla_\Sigma z|=\sqrt{1-N_z^2}.$$ Its mean square over the surface patch will be denoted by $$b_z=\frac1A\int_{\Sigma\cap Q}(1-N_z^2)\,dS.$$ All section identities at interior heights are initially understood at regular levels.

<a id="stmt-slice-regularity"></a>
<div id="slice:regularity" class="lemma">

<a id="slice:substitution"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Regular levels and area coordinates). *There are only finitely many exceptional axial heights. The function $v$ is continuous, nonincreasing, absolutely continuous, and smooth off those heights. The end levels are regular, with a regular collar whenever nonempty. For every nonnegative measurable function $\gamma$, $$\int_0^1 \gamma(v(z))(-v'(z))\,dz
       =\int_r^s\gamma(w)\,dw.
 \label{slice:substitution}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-slice-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-slice-regularity</summary>

*Proof.* A critical point of the height on $\Sigma$ has $N=\pm e_z$. By <a href="#geo:strict-normal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:strict-normal">[geo:strict-normal]</a>, both $x,y$ then belong to $\{0,1\}$. Such a point lies on an axial edge, which is transverse to the surface. These intersections are isolated and, by compactness and the exclusion of vertices, finite.

The boundary faces of the square introduce no further exceptional heights. On $x=c$, $c\in\{0,1\}$, one has $N_x=0$; a tangent to the face intersection is $e_x\times N=(0,-N_z,N_y)$. Its height derivative can vanish only if $N_y=0$, forcing $y=0$ or $1$, and hence an already-counted axial edge. Interchanging $x,y$ treats the other faces. Equivalently, reflect in these two coordinates and take sections in a transverse two-torus.

At the end faces $N_z=0$, so the height restricted to the reflected surface is regular there. Compactness supplies a regular collar; an empty end section has an empty collar. At any interior height the intersection with $\Sigma$ is a union of regular curve pieces and finitely many critical points, of zero planar area. Any jump of the nested section areas would be supported on that boundary intersection. Thus $v$ is continuous. On intervals of regular levels it is smooth by smooth transport of the compact section curves.

Monotonicity makes $Dv$ a finite measure. Its singular part can be supported only on the finite set of exceptional heights. Continuity rules out atoms, and therefore this singular part is zero. This proves absolute continuity without first assuming integrability of $1/\alpha$.

For <a href="#slice:substitution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:substitution">[slice:substitution]</a>, push forward the measure $(-v'(z))\,dz$ under $v$. For $r<a<b<s$, the inverse image of $(a,b)$ is an interval, up to endpoint plateaus, and absolute continuity gives it measure $b-a$. A level set has zero measure for this pushforward: on any plateau $v'=0$ almost everywhere. The pushforward is therefore Lebesgue measure on $(r,s)$. The assertion follows first for interval indicators and then for all nonnegative measurable $\gamma$. No regularity of the inverse of $v$ is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-slice-identities"></a>
<div id="slice:identities" class="lemma">

<a id="slice:flux"></a>
<a id="slice:projection"></a>
<a id="slice:coarea"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Flux and coarea). *With the preceding notation, $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\partial E_z}\alpha\,d\ell
     &=b_z A+h(v(z)-V),\notag\\
 p(0)&=b_zA+h(s-V),&
 p(1)&=b_zA+h(r-V),
 \label{slice:flux}\\
 s-r&=\int_{\Sigma\cap Q}N_z\,dS
       \le A\sqrt{1-b_z},
 \label{slice:projection}\\
 -v'(z)&=\int_{\partial E_z}\frac{N_z}{\alpha}\,d\ell,&
 A&=\int_0^1\int_{\partial E_z}\frac1\alpha\,d\ell\,dz.
 \label{slice:coarea}
\end{aligned}$$ Moreover $b_x+b_y+b_z=2$, and $b_i<1$ in every direction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-slice-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-slice-identities</summary>

*Proof.* Apply first variation to $X=\eta(z)e_z$, where $\eta\in C_c^\infty(0,1)$. Orthogonality to the cube faces eliminates the boundary term; the same computation may be made after reflection. The outward/sum-curvature convention and the divergence theorem give $$\int_{\Sigma\cap Q}\eta'(z)(1-N_z^2)\,dS
       =h\int_{\Sigma\cap Q}\eta(z)N_z\,dS
       =h\int_0^1\eta'(z)v(z)\,dz.$$ Coarea converts the first integral to $\int\eta'(z)\int_{\partial E_z}\alpha\,d\ell\,dz$. Consequently $\int_{\partial E_z}\alpha\,d\ell-hv(z)$ is constant. Averaging over the unit height interval identifies it as $b_zA-hV$. Continuity on regular intervals gives the identity at every regular level. At each end, $\alpha=1$, and the regular collar gives the two endpoint formulas in <a href="#slice:flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:flux">[slice:flux]</a>.

The divergence theorem for $e_z$ gives $\int_{\Sigma\cap Q}N_z\,dS=s-r$. Since $\int N_z^2\,dS=A(1-b_z)$, Cauchy–Schwarz yields <a href="#slice:projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:projection">[slice:projection]</a>.

At a regular height, the outward planar normal velocity of the slice curve is $-N_z/\alpha$. The area-variation formula gives the first identity in <a href="#slice:coarea" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:coarea">[slice:coarea]</a>. Coarea on the surface gives the second; the finite critical-point set has surface area zero, so no term is lost there. These integrals remain valid if their integrands become unbounded as an exceptional height is approached.

Finally $\sum_i(1-N_i^2)=2$ pointwise, giving the sum of the $b_i$. The strict sign of $N_i$ on a nonempty interior patch gives $\int N_i^2>0$, hence $b_i<1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="slice:early-exclusions"></a>
### The first endpoint exclusions

We now apply the section identities to the two vertex patterns in Proposition <a href="#geo:patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:patterns">8</a>. The four-vertex pattern is excluded at both transition volumes by the same estimate. The three-vertex pattern is excluded at the first transition; at the second, the identities give the parameters needed for the nesting argument.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The four-vertex pattern

<a id="stmt-slice-four-vertex"></a>
<div id="slice:four-vertex" class="proposition">

**Proposition 11**. *A nonstandard minimizer at either $V_1=4\pi/81$ or $V_2=1/\pi$ cannot have the occupied vertex pattern $\{000,100,010,001\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-slice-four-vertex"><summary>Proof of stmt-slice-four-vertex</summary>

<a id="slice:four-parameters"></a>
<a id="slice:four-endpoint-inequalities"></a>
*Proof.* The complement of this pattern becomes the same pattern after reversing all three coordinates. We may therefore choose the smaller-volume side, so that $V\in\{V_1,V_2\}$ and $h\ge0$. Put $$a=\sqrt{\pi V},\qquad x=hA.$$ The standard competitors and the curvature estimate <a href="#pre:curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:curvature">[pre:curvature]</a> give $$\label{slice:four-parameters}
 0<A\le a\le1,\qquad
 0\le x\le\sqrt{\pi A}\le\sqrt\pi.$$ Since $b_x+b_y+b_z=2$, choose a slicing direction, denoted by $z$, for which $b=b_z\ge2/3$, and write $d=\sqrt{1-b}$. The strict positivity of $N_z$ in the interior implies $b<1$, so $$0<d\le\frac1{\sqrt3}.$$ The top section is a corner region, separated from the two opposite edges of its square. The bottom section has a complement of the same kind at the opposite corner. Reflecting either corner region in its two adjacent edges gives a planar region of four times its area and four times its relative perimeter. The planar isoperimetric inequality therefore gives $$p(1)\ge\sqrt{\pi r},\qquad
 p(0)\ge\sqrt{\pi(1-s)}.$$ Monotonicity gives $r\le V$. Define $w\in[0,1]$ by $\sqrt{\pi r}=aw$. The endpoint flux identities <a href="#slice:flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:flux">[slice:flux]</a> and the projection estimate <a href="#slice:projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:projection">[slice:projection]</a> now imply $$\label{slice:four-endpoint-inequalities}
\begin{aligned}
 aw&\le Ab-\frac{ha^2}{\pi}(1-w^2),\\
 \sqrt{\pi(1-s)}
 &\le Ab-\frac{ha^2}{\pi}(1-w^2)+xd,\\
 s&\le\frac{a^2w^2}{\pi}+Ad.
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, $p(0)=p(1)+h(s-r)$, and $s-r\le Ad$.

<a id="slice:four-root"></a>
<a id="slice:four-contradiction"></a>
Dividing the first inequality in <a href="#slice:four-endpoint-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-endpoint-inequalities">[slice:four-endpoint-inequalities]</a> by $a$ and using $A\le a$ and $ha\ge x$ yields $$f(w):=w+\frac{x}{\pi}(1-w^2)\le b.$$ Since $f(1)=1>b$, continuity gives $\tau\in[w,1)$ with $$\label{slice:four-root}
 \tau+\frac{x}{\pi}(1-\tau^2)=1-d^2,
 \qquad
 x=\frac{\pi(1-\tau-d^2)}{1-\tau^2}.$$ In particular $\tau\le1-d^2$. No monotonicity of $f$ is required. The function $E(q)=Ab-ha^2(1-q^2)/\pi$ is nondecreasing for $q\ge0$, and $$E(\tau)
 =A\tau+\frac{h(A^2-a^2)}{\pi}(1-\tau^2)
 \le a\tau.$$ Consequently <a href="#slice:four-endpoint-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-endpoint-inequalities">[slice:four-endpoint-inequalities]</a> gives $$\sqrt{\pi(1-s)}\le a\tau+xd,
 \qquad
 s\le\frac{a^2\tau^2}{\pi}+Ad.$$ Both sides of the first inequality are nonnegative. Squaring it, combining the two inequalities, and using $A,a\le1$, we obtain the necessary condition $$\label{slice:four-contradiction}
 1\le R(\tau,d):=
 d+\frac{\tau^2+(\tau+xd)^2}{\pi}.$$ The scalar inequality in Lemma <a href="#num:four-vertex-scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:four-vertex-scalar">21</a> contradicts <a href="#slice:four-contradiction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-contradiction">[slice:four-contradiction]</a>; its hypotheses follow from <a href="#slice:four-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-parameters">[slice:four-parameters]</a> and <a href="#slice:four-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-root">[slice:four-root]</a>, together with $0<d\le1/\sqrt3$. This excludes the four-vertex pattern at both transition volumes. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The three-vertex pattern

<a id="slice:three-flux"></a>
Consider the pattern $\{000,100,010\}$, with $z$ the missing axial direction. At this stage the occupied side may a priori be either side of the original minimizer. The top square is vacant, and is separated from the region by a nonempty collar, so $v(z)=p(z)=0$ near $z=1$. The flux constant in <a href="#slice:flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:flux">[slice:flux]</a> is therefore zero: $b_zA=hV$. The bottom section has a corner complement and $0<s<1$. Its planar perimeter bound and <a href="#slice:projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:projection">[slice:projection]</a> give $$\label{slice:three-flux}
 \int\alpha\,d\ell=hv(z),\qquad
 p(0)=hs\ge\sqrt{\pi(1-s)},\qquad
 s^2\le A(A-hV).$$ The first identity holds at every regular slicing level. In particular $h>0$, since the bottom complement has positive area. Profile concavity and complement symmetry give nonpositive outward curvature on the larger-volume side. Thus this pattern necessarily labels the smaller-volume side, and $V$ equals the endpoint volume under consideration.

<a id="stmt-slice-three-first"></a>
<div id="slice:three-first" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12**. *A nonstandard minimizer at $V_1=4\pi/81$ cannot have the three-vertex pattern.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-slice-three-first"><summary>Proof of stmt-slice-three-first</summary>

<a id="slice:three-first-scalar"></a>
*Proof.* Set $a=\sqrt{\pi V_1}=2\pi/9$ and $x=hA$. As before, $A\le a$ and $0\le x\le M:=\sqrt{\pi a}=\pi\sqrt2/3<\pi$. Since $V_1=a^2/\pi\ge A^2/\pi$, <a href="#slice:three-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-flux">[slice:three-flux]</a> gives $$s^2\le A^2-xV_1\le A^2(1-x/\pi),
 \qquad s\le A\sqrt{1-x/\pi}.$$ Combining this with $\pi(1-s)\le h^2s^2$ yields $$\label{slice:three-first-scalar}
 1\le a\sqrt{1-x/\pi}
       +\frac{x^2}{\pi}(1-x/\pi).$$ The first summand decreases in $x$. The second, denoted by $g(x)$, has derivative $g'(x)=x(2\pi-3x)/\pi^2\ge0$ on $[0,M]$, since $M<2\pi/3$. For $0\le x\le1$, the right side of <a href="#slice:three-first-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-first-scalar">[slice:three-first-scalar]</a> is at most $$a+g(1)
 <\frac{44}{63}+\frac29=\frac{58}{63}<1.$$ Here $(\pi-1)/\pi^2<2/9$ because $(p-1)/p^2$ decreases for $p>2$ and $\pi>3$. For $1\le x\le M$, it is at most $$\begin{aligned}
 a\sqrt{1-1/\pi}+g(M)
 &=a\left(\sqrt{1-1/\pi}+1-\frac{\sqrt2}{3}\right)\\
 &<\frac{44}{63}\left(\frac56+\frac8{15}\right)
 =\frac{902}{945}<1.
\end{aligned}$$ The strict bounds $\sqrt{1-1/\pi}<5/6$ and $\sqrt2>7/5$ follow by squaring, using $\pi<22/7$. Both intervals contradict <a href="#slice:three-first-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-first-scalar">[slice:three-first-scalar]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-slice-three-second-parameters"></a>
<div id="slice:three-second-parameters" class="proposition">

<a id="slice:three-parameters"></a>
<a id="slice:t-definition"></a>
**Proposition 13**. *Suppose that a nonstandard minimizer at $V_2=1/\pi$ has the three-vertex pattern. Then $A\le1$, the identities <a href="#slice:three-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-flux">[slice:three-flux]</a> hold, and $$\label{slice:three-parameters}
 \frac{\sqrt{\pi(1-s)}}s\le h\le\pi(1-s^2),\qquad
 0.535<s<0.89,\qquad 0.64\le h\le2.25,\qquad s\ge t(h),$$ where $$\label{slice:t-definition}
 t=t(h):=\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{1+4h^2/\pi}},
 \qquad h^2t^2=\pi(1-t).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-slice-three-second-parameters"><summary>Proof of stmt-slice-three-second-parameters</summary>

*Proof.* The slab competitor gives $A\le1$. The lower bound on $h$ in <a href="#slice:three-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-parameters">[slice:three-parameters]</a> follows from the bottom perimeter inequality in <a href="#slice:three-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-flux">[slice:three-flux]</a>. Its projection inequality gives $$h\le\pi\left(A-\frac{s^2}{A}\right)
 \le\pi(1-s^2),$$ since $A-s^2/A$ is increasing for $A>0$. Solving $h^2s^2+\pi s-\pi\ge0$ for $s>0$ gives $s\ge t(h)$.

Combining the two bounds on $h$, and dividing by $1-s>0$, gives $$\Phi(s):=s^2(1-s)(1+s)^2\ge\frac1\pi.$$ Its derivative is $\Phi'(s)=s(1+s)(2+s-5s^2)$, so $\Phi$ increases up to $s_*=(1+\sqrt{41})/10$ and decreases thereafter. The points $107/200$ and $89/100$ lie on the respective sides of $s_*$. Exact arithmetic gives $$\frac{22}{7}\Phi(107/200)
 =\frac{1103875107423}{1120000000000}<1,
 \qquad
 \frac{22}{7}\Phi(89/100)
 =\frac{4890924423}{5000000000}<1.$$ Since $\pi<22/7$, unimodality implies $107/200<s<89/100$. Finally, $\sqrt{\pi(1-s)}/s$ decreases with $s$, and hence $$h>\sqrt{\frac{3300}{7921}}>\frac{16}{25},
 \qquad
 h<\frac{22}{7}\left(1-\left(\frac{107}{200}\right)^2\right)
 =\frac{314061}{140000}<\frac94.$$ For the first strict comparison, $\pi>3$ was used; its last step follows from $3300/7921-(16/25)^2=34724/4950625>0$. These strict bounds imply the closed curvature interval stated in <a href="#slice:three-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-parameters">[slice:three-parameters]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="nest:section"></a>
### Slice nesting at the cylinder–slab transition

We consider the three-vertex pattern at volume $V=1/\pi$, under the contradictory assumption $A\leq1$. The slicing direction is the missing axis, so that the top slices are empty. Proposition <a href="#slice:three-second-parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="slice:three-second-parameters">13</a> gives $0.64\leq h\leq2.25$ and $s\geq t=t(h)$, where <a href="#slice:t-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:t-definition">[slice:t-definition]</a> defines $t$ by $h^2t^2=\pi(1-t)$. In particular, $h>0$ and $0<t<1$. We use the flux identity $$\int_{\partial E_z}\alpha\,d\ell=hv(z),\qquad p(0)=hs,$$ from <a href="#slice:three-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:three-flux">[slice:three-flux]</a>, and the coarea identities <a href="#slice:coarea" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:coarea">[slice:coarea]</a>. All perimeters in this Section are relative to the unit square. All terminating decimals denote exact rational numbers.

The argument first gives a lower bound depending only on $h$ and $s$. It then improves that bound on an interval of slice areas by retaining the fact that every slice is contained in the bottom slice. This is especially useful when a slice changes from a strip to the complement of a corner region.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### An integrated flux inequality

Fix $\lambda=0.98$. Subtracting $\lambda$ times the flux from the surface-area coarea density gives $1/\alpha-\lambda\alpha$, whereas the decrease of slice area has density $\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}/\alpha$. At a constant value $0<\alpha<1$, their ratio is $g(\alpha)$, where $$g(X)=\frac{1-\lambda X^2}{\sqrt{1-X^2}},\qquad 0\leq X<1.$$ The flux determines only the arclength mean $hv/p$ of $\alpha$, not its pointwise values. The next lemma uses convexity to obtain a whole-slice comparison from this mean. To make that comparison compatible with perimeter lower bounds, we use a non-increasing minorant of $g$: replacing $p$ by a smaller quantity increases the argument $hv/p$ and does not increase the resulting lower bound.

<a id="nest:weight"></a>
<a id="nest:weight-derivative"></a>
Set $$\label{nest:weight}
 \begin{gathered}
 D=\frac{\sqrt{48}}7,\qquad \ell=0.28,\\
 G(X)=g(X_0),\qquad X_0=\min\{X,D\},\quad X\geq0.
 \end{gathered}$$ Direct differentiation gives $$\label{nest:weight-derivative}
 -\frac{d}{dr}g(\sqrt r)
   =\frac{0.48-0.49r}{(1-r)^{3/2}},\qquad 0\leq r<1.$$ Thus $g$ decreases up to $D$, increases thereafter, and $g(D)=7/25=\ell$. Consequently $G$ is non-increasing, takes values in $[\ell,1]$, and satisfies $G(X)\leq g(X)$ for $0\leq X<1$. Its definition for $X\geq1$ also covers perimeter lower bounds for which the substituted argument exceeds one.

<a id="stmt-nest-integrated-flux"></a>
<div id="nest:integrated-flux" class="lemma">

<a id="nest:jensen"></a>
<a id="nest:area-coordinate"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Integrated flux). *At every nonempty regular slice, with area $v$ and perimeter $p$, $$\label{nest:jensen}
 \int_{\partial E_z}\frac{d\ell}{\alpha}-\lambda hv
 \geq
 G(hv/p)\int_{\partial E_z}
       \frac{\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}}{\alpha}\,d\ell.$$ If a nonnegative measurable function $\gamma$ satisfies $G(hv(z)/p(z))\geq\gamma(v(z))$ at the nonempty regular levels, then $$\label{nest:area-coordinate}
 A\geq\frac{\lambda h}{\pi}+\int_0^s\gamma(v)\,dv.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-integrated-flux"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-integrated-flux</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed $J\in[0,1]$, let $$f_J(X)=\frac1X-\lambda X-J\frac{\sqrt{1-X^2}}X,
 \qquad 0<X\leq1.$$ On $(0,1)$, $$X^3f_J''(X)=2-J\frac{2-3X^2}{(1-X^2)^{3/2}}\geq0.$$ Indeed, the function $(2-3r)/(1-r)^{3/2}$ decreases from $2$ to $0$ on $[0,2/3]$, its derivative being $-3r/[2(1-r)^{5/2}]$; it is nonpositive on $[2/3,1)$. Convexity extends to the continuous endpoint value at $X=1$.

Use normalized arclength on the slice curve as a probability measure. The flux identity says that the mean of $\alpha$ is $m=hv/p\in(0,1]$. Take $J=G(m)$. For $m<1$, $$f_J(m)=\frac{\sqrt{1-m^2}}m\bigl(g(m)-G(m)\bigr)\geq0;$$ for $m=1$, $f_J(1)=1-\lambda>0$. Jensen’s inequality now yields $\int f_J(\alpha)\,d\ell\geq0$, which is <a href="#nest:jensen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:jensen">[nest:jensen]</a>.

Down-set position gives $N_z\geq0$, hence $\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}=N_z$. The coarea identities therefore turn <a href="#nest:jensen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:jensen">[nest:jensen]</a> into $$A-\lambda hV
 \geq\int_0^1G(hv(z)/p(z))\,(-v'(z))\,dz
 \geq\int_0^1\gamma(v(z))\,(-v'(z))\,dz.$$ The integrands are set to zero at empty or exceptional levels. Lemma <a href="#slice:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="slice:regularity">9</a>, with $r=0$, identifies the last integral with $\int_0^s\gamma(v)\,dv$. Since $V=1/\pi$, this proves <a href="#nest:area-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:area-coordinate">[nest:area-coordinate]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-nest-planar-patterns"></a>
<div id="nest:planar-patterns" class="lemma">

<a id="nest:planar-bound"></a>
<a id="nest:baseline"></a>
<a id="nest:baseline-slack"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Planar patterns and the baseline bound). *Every nonempty regular slice satisfies $$\label{nest:planar-bound}
 p\geq p_*(v):=
 \min\{\sqrt{\pi v},\ 1,\ \sqrt{\pi(1-v)}\}.$$ Put $a_0=1/\pi$, $b_0=1-1/\pi$, and define $$\label{nest:baseline}
 B(h)=\frac{\lambda h}{\pi}
       +\int_0^{t(h)}G\!\left(\frac{hv}{p_*(v)}\right)\,dv-1.$$ At $v=0$ the argument in the integrand is understood by its limit. Then $$\label{nest:baseline-slack}
 A-1\geq B(h)+\ell(s-t).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-planar-patterns"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-planar-patterns</summary>

*Proof.* The far vertex $(1,1)$ is absent even from the bottom slice and hence from every slice. A nonempty regular slice has, by monotonicity, one, two adjacent, or three occupied square vertices. In the first case the slice is confined to the occupied corner. Reflection across its two adjacent sides gives a bounded planar set of area $4v$ and perimeter $4p$; the Euclidean isoperimetric inequality gives $p^2\geq\pi v$. In the three-vertex case the same argument applies to the opposite-corner complement and gives $p^2\geq\pi(1-v)$.

In the two-vertex case, after interchanging the square coordinates if necessary, the occupied vertices form the bottom edge. This entire edge is occupied and the opposite edge is vacant. Each vertical segment crosses the separating curve exactly once: monotonicity gives uniqueness, and the strict normal inequality gives a transverse crossing. The implicit function theorem, also in the reflected endpoint neighborhoods, writes the curve as a single decreasing graph $y=f(x)$ for $0\leq x\leq1$. Its length is at least $1$. These arguments establish <a href="#nest:planar-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:planar-bound">[nest:planar-bound]</a>, including both strip orientations.

Since $G$ is non-increasing, <a href="#nest:planar-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:planar-bound">[nest:planar-bound]</a> implies $G(hv/p)\geq G(hv/p_*(v))$. Apply Lemma <a href="#nest:integrated-flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:integrated-flux">14</a> and use $s\geq t$ and $G\geq\ell$ on the remaining interval $[t,s]$ to obtain <a href="#nest:baseline-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:baseline-slack">[nest:baseline-slack]</a>. ◻

</details>

The scalar estimates in Appendix <a href="#num:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:section">9</a> show that $B(h)$ suffices outside $[1.02,1.62]$ but is negative at $h=1.46$. The slack $s-t$ contributes $\ell(s-t)$ directly. When this slack is small, the bottom complement constrains both edge extents; nesting then bounds each strip’s smaller endpoint and forces a length excess. We quantify these two gains and minimize their sum over the slack.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The bottom slice controls both strip orientations

In the remainder of the geometric improvement assume $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$. Let $L_x,L_y$ be the extents of the bottom complement along the sides $y=1,x=1$, respectively. They are measured from its corner $(1,1)$ and need not be equal. Figure <a href="#nest:containment-figure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:containment-figure">2</a> shows how each extent constrains the endpoint of a contained strip in the corresponding orientation.

<figure id="nest:containment-figure">

<figcaption>Nesting constrains a strip through the bottom complement. The shaded strip $E_{z} = {(x,y) : 0 ≤ y ≤ f(x)}$ lies in $E_{0}$, so its smaller endpoint satisfies $f(1) ≤ 1 − L_{y}$. Interchanging $x$ and $y$ gives the bound using $L_{x}$ for the other strip orientation. The curves and the unequal extents are schematic; no convexity or circular shape is assumed.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-nest-containment-figure"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`nest:containment-figure`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=5cm,y=5cm,>=stealth,line cap=round,line join=round]
 \fill[black!12] (0.36,1) -- (1,1) -- (1,0.28)
   .. controls (0.76,0.28) and (0.56,0.43) .. (0.51,0.61)
   .. controls (0.47,0.78) and (0.36,0.83) .. (0.36,1) -- cycle;
 \fill[blue!12] (0,0) -- (1,0) -- (1,0.18)
   .. controls (0.65,0.18) and (0.38,0.63) .. (0,0.63) -- cycle;
 \draw[black!65] (0,0) rectangle (1,1);
 \draw[thick] (0.36,1)
   .. controls (0.36,0.83) and (0.47,0.78) .. (0.51,0.61)
   .. controls (0.56,0.43) and (0.76,0.28) .. (1,0.28);
 \draw[thick,blue!60!black] (0,0.63)
   .. controls (0.38,0.63) and (0.65,0.18) .. (1,0.18);
 \draw[<->] (0.36,1.06) -- node[above] {$L_x$} (1,1.06);
 \draw[<->] (1.07,0.28) -- node[right] {$L_y$} (1.07,1);
 \draw[densely dotted,black!65] (0,0.28) -- (1,0.28);
 \node[left] at (0,0.28) {$1-L_y$};
 \fill (1,0.28) circle[radius=0.009];
 \fill[blue!60!black] (1,0.18) circle[radius=0.009];
 \draw[blue!60!black] (1,0.18) -- (1.18,0.11);
 \node[right,blue!60!black] at (1.18,0.11) {$f(1)$};
 \node at (0.79,0.80) {$[0,1]^2\setminus E_0$};
 \node[blue!60!black] at (0.29,0.19) {$E_z$};
 \node at (0.24,0.82) {$E_0$};
 \node[blue!60!black,above] at (0.24,0.575) {$y=f(x)$};
 \node[below left] at (0,0) {$0$};
 \node[below] at (1,0) {$1$};
 \node[left] at (0,1) {$1$};
 \node[below] at (0.52,-0.03) {$x$};
 \node[left] at (-0.03,0.62) {$y$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 15 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

A 2D diagram on the unit square $[0, 1]^2$. Horizontal axis labeled $x$ with ticks at 0 and 1; vertical axis labeled $y$ with ticks at 0 and 1. Above the top edge, a dimension arrow spans width $L_x$; along the right edge, a dimension arrow spans height $L_y$. A dashed horizontal line indicates the height $1 - L_y$ on the y-axis. <a id="stmt-nest-bottom-calibration"></a>
<div id="nest:bottom-calibration" class="lemma">

<a id="nest:extent-bound"></a>
<a id="nest:endpoint-bound"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Bottom calibration and nesting). *Each $L\in\{L_x,L_y\}$ satisfies $$\label{nest:extent-bound}
 hs\geq\frac{1-s}{L}+\frac{\pi L}{4}.$$ Consequently, a strip slice, written as a decreasing subgraph across either square coordinate, has smaller endpoint at most $$\label{nest:endpoint-bound}
 \begin{split}
 e&=1-\frac2\pi
       \left(hs-\sqrt{h^2s^2-\pi(1-s)}\right)\\
  &\leq1-\frac{2ht}{\pi}+QY,
 \qquad
 Y=\sqrt{s-t},\qquad
 Q=\frac2\pi\sqrt{\pi+2h^2t}.
 \end{split}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-bottom-calibration"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-bottom-calibration</summary>

*Proof.* Use coordinates $(\xi,\eta)$ measured from the complement’s corner, so that its monotone separating curve runs from $(0,L_y)$ to $(L_x,0)$ with $d\xi\geq0$, $d\eta\leq0$. For $L=L_y$, the vector $$\left(\frac\eta L,-\sqrt{1-\frac{\eta^2}{L^2}}\right)$$ has unit length. Pairing it with the tangent differential and integrating gives $$\begin{split}
 hs&\geq
 \frac1L\int\eta\,d\xi
   -\int\sqrt{1-\eta^2/L^2}\,d\eta\\
 &=\frac{1-s}{L}+\int_0^L\sqrt{1-\eta^2/L^2}\,d\eta
 =\frac{1-s}{L}+\frac{\pi L}4.
 \end{split}$$ The first integral is the complement area. This calculation uses only monotonicity of the curve, and so requires no convexity. Exchanging $\xi$ and $\eta$ proves the same estimate for $L_x$. Both extents are positive, because the complement has positive area.

Solving the quadratic inequality <a href="#nest:extent-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:extent-bound">[nest:extent-bound]</a> gives, for each extent, $$L\geq\frac2\pi
    \left(hs-\sqrt{h^2s^2-\pi(1-s)}\right).$$ The discriminant is nonnegative by $hs\geq\sqrt{\pi(1-s)}$. A strip $y=f(x)$ is contained in the bottom slice, so its smaller endpoint satisfies $f(1)\leq1-L_y\leq e$. For a strip $x=f(y)$ the same argument uses $L_x$. Thus one common bound controls both orientations without imposing a symmetry on the two extents.

Finally, write $s=t+Y^2$ and use $h^2t^2=\pi(1-t)$ to obtain $$h^2s^2-\pi(1-s)
   =(\pi+2h^2t)Y^2+h^2Y^4.$$ Its square root is at most $\sqrt{\pi+2h^2t}\,Y+hY^2$. Substituting this upper bound in $e$ cancels the term $hY^2$ coming from $hs$, and proves <a href="#nest:endpoint-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:endpoint-bound">[nest:endpoint-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

We seek this excess on an interval $[u-\delta,u]$ within the middle branch of $p_*$; the choice of $u$ also keeps $hv\leq0.96<D$. With $Q,Y$ as in <a href="#nest:endpoint-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:endpoint-bound">[nest:endpoint-bound]</a>, set <a id="nest:gain-parameters"></a>$$\label{nest:gain-parameters}
 \begin{gathered}
 u=\min\{b_0,0.96/h\},\qquad
 P=u-1+\frac{2ht}{\pi},\\
 d_m=0.21,\qquad c=2.7,\qquad
 \delta=\min\{d_m,(P-QY)_+\},
 \end{gathered}$$ where $x_+=\max\{x,0\}$. Here $P-QY$ is a lower bound for the gap between $u$ and the smaller strip endpoint, and $\delta$ is its positive part capped at $d_m$.

<a id="stmt-nest-shape-gain"></a>
<div id="nest:shape-gain" class="lemma">

<a id="nest:interval-placement"></a>
<a id="nest:improved-perimeter"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Placement and the three slice types). *On $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$ one has $$\label{nest:interval-placement}
 u-d_m\geq\frac{1033}{2700}>0.38>a_0,
 \qquad u\leq b_0,\qquad u<t\leq s,\qquad hu\leq0.96.$$ Define <a id="nest:shape-function"></a>$$\label{nest:shape-function}
 H(q)=\min\left\{q^2,\frac{\pi(1-q)}{c d_m}\right\},
 \qquad 0\leq q\leq1.$$ If $\delta>0$, put $q=(v-u+\delta)/\delta$ for $u-\delta\leq v\leq u$. Every regular slice in this area interval satisfies $$\label{nest:improved-perimeter}
 p^2\geq1+c\delta^2H(q).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-shape-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-shape-gain</summary>

<a id="nest:strip-endpoint"></a>
*Proof.* The definition of $u$ gives $u\geq0.96/1.62=16/27$, $u\leq b_0$, and $hu\leq0.96$. Hence the first assertion follows from $16/27-0.21=1033/2700$, using $\pi>3$. Moreover, $$h^2u^2\leq0.96^2<1\leq\pi(1-u).$$ The function $x\mapsto h^2x^2+\pi x-\pi$ is strictly increasing for $x\geq0$ and vanishes at $t$, so $u<t$. This proves <a href="#nest:interval-placement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:interval-placement">[nest:interval-placement]</a>; in particular $[u-\delta,u]\subset[a_0,b_0]\cap[0,s]$, where $p_*=1$. For the remaining assertion assume $\delta>0$. The definition of $\delta$ and <a href="#nest:endpoint-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:endpoint-bound">[nest:endpoint-bound]</a> then give $$\label{nest:strip-endpoint}
 e\leq u-P+QY\leq u-\delta.$$

We verify <a href="#nest:improved-perimeter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:improved-perimeter">[nest:improved-perimeter]</a> separately for all three slice types of Lemma <a href="#nest:planar-patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:planar-patterns">15</a>.

*Occupied corner.* Here $p^2\geq\pi v$. Since $v>0.38$ and $\pi>3$, $$p^2-1>0.14>c d_m^2=0.11907\geq c\delta^2H(q).$$ In the last step we used $H(q)\leq q^2\leq1$.

*Opposite-corner complement.* Here $p^2\geq\pi(1-v)$, and $v=u-\delta+\delta q$ gives $$\begin{split}
 p^2-1&\geq\pi(b_0-v)
 \geq\pi\delta(1-q)\\
 &\geq c\delta^2\frac{\pi(1-q)}{c d_m}
 \geq c\delta^2H(q).
 \end{split}$$ This includes the endpoint $q=1$, $u=b_0$, where $H(1)=0$.

<a id="nest:strip-calibration"></a>
*Strip.* Write the strip as $y=f(x)$, interchanging the two square coordinates if required. Its smooth decreasing graph has $f(1)\leq e$ by Lemma <a href="#nest:bottom-calibration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:bottom-calibration">16</a>. Integration by parts and <a href="#nest:strip-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:strip-endpoint">[nest:strip-endpoint]</a> give $$\int_0^1x|df|=\int_0^1f(x)\,dx-f(1)
    =v-f(1)\geq v-u+\delta=:j=\delta q.$$ Set $k=2.5j\leq2.5d_m=0.525$. Pair the graph tangent $(dx,|df|)$ with the unit vector $(\sqrt{1-k^2x^2},kx)$ to obtain $$\label{nest:strip-calibration}
 p\geq kj+\int_0^1\sqrt{1-k^2x^2}\,dx.$$ For $0\leq w\leq0.525^2$, $$\sqrt{1-w}\geq1-\frac w2-\frac{w^2}5.$$ Indeed the right side is positive and its square minus $1-w$ is $w^2(-3/20+w/5+w^2/25)\leq0$; the expression in parentheses is increasing and is negative at $w=0.525^2$. It follows from <a href="#nest:strip-calibration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:strip-calibration">[nest:strip-calibration]</a> that $$\begin{split}
 p&\geq1+\left(\frac{35}{24}-\frac{25j^2}{16}\right)j^2\\
  &\geq1+\frac{26677}{19200}j^2
   \geq1+1.35j^2.
 \end{split}$$ Squaring gives $p^2\geq1+2.7j^2\geq1+c\delta^2H(q)$. This establishes the same estimate for every slice type, even if the type or strip orientation changes within the area interval. Exceptional levels are not needed in this argument and have zero measure after the substitution in Lemma <a href="#nest:integrated-flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:integrated-flux">14</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### From perimeter improvement to an area gain

The perimeter improvement has size proportional to $\delta^2$ on an interval of slice areas of width $\delta$. We will show that it adds an explicit positive multiple of $\delta^3$ to the total area bound. The derivative estimate below converts the improvement of $p$ into an improvement of $G(hv/p)$; the moments of $H$ then average that improvement over the interval. Together with the already obtained term $\ell(s-t)$, this will leave a one-variable minimization over the unknown bottom area $s$.

<a id="stmt-nest-derivative-bound"></a>
<div id="nest:derivative-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Derivative estimate). *For $0\leq X\leq0.96$, <a id="nest:derivative-inequality"></a>$$\label{nest:derivative-inequality}
 \frac{0.48-0.49X^2}{(1-X^2)^{3/2}}\geq0.91X.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-derivative-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-derivative-bound</summary>

*Proof.* The assertion is immediate at $X=0$. For $X>0$, divide the left side by $X$ and write the result as $$\frac{N(X)}{D_1(X)},\qquad
 N(X)=0.49-\frac{0.01}{1-X^2},\qquad
 D_1(X)=X\sqrt{1-X^2}.$$ The numerator decreases; the denominator is at most $1/2$ everywhere and decreases for $X\geq1/\sqrt2$. The following rational bounds therefore suffice: $$\begin{array}{c|cc}
 \text{interval for }X&N(X)\text{ lower bound}&D_1(X)\text{ upper bound}\\
 \hline
 {[0,0.84]}&0.456&0.50\\
 {[0.84,0.89]}&0.44&0.46\\
 {[0.89,0.94]}&0.40&0.41\\
 {[0.94,0.96]}&0.36&0.33
\end{array}$$ For clarity, each entry is certified by arithmetic on rational numbers. If $b$ is the right endpoint and $n$ the numerator bound, the four values of $(0.49-n)(1-b^2)-0.01$ are respectively $0.0000096$, $0.000395$, $0.000476$, $0.000192$, all positive. For the last three rows the values of $D_1(a)^2$ at the left endpoints are $0.20772864$, $0.16467759$, $0.10285104$, smaller than $0.46^2$, $0.41^2$, $0.33^2$ respectively. Finally each numerator lower bound exceeds $0.91$ times the denominator upper bound; the respective differences are $0.001$, $0.0214$, $0.0269$, $0.0597$. ◻

</details>

<a id="nest:shape-moments"></a>
Define the moments and a perimeter bound associated with $H$ by $$\label{nest:shape-moments}
 M=\int_0^1H(q)\,dq,\qquad
 q_m=\frac{\int_0^1qH(q)\,dq}{M},\qquad
 p_2=1+c d_m^2\max_{[0,1]}H.$$

<a id="stmt-nest-coefficient-bound"></a>
<div id="nest:coefficient-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="nest:moment-bounds"></a>
<a id="nest:coefficient-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Uniform coefficient). *The constants in <a href="#nest:shape-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:shape-moments">[nest:shape-moments]</a> satisfy $$\label{nest:moment-bounds}
 M>0.2659,\qquad q_m>0.697,\qquad p_2<1.09.$$ For $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$, $$\label{nest:coefficient-inequality}
 \frac{0.91cM}{p_2^{3/2}}
       \bigl[h(u-(1-q_m)d_m)\bigr]^3
 \geq C_0(h),$$ where <a id="nest:gain-coefficient"></a>$$\label{nest:gain-coefficient}
 C_0(h)=\min\{0.134h^3,0.36\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-coefficient-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-coefficient-bound</summary>

*Proof.* The two branches of $H$ meet once, at $$\theta=\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{1+4cd_m/\pi}},
 \qquad cd_m\theta^2=\pi(1-\theta).$$ Thus $H(q)=q^2$ up to $\theta$ and $H(q)=\pi(1-q)/(cd_m)$ thereafter, with maximum $\theta^2$. Integration, followed by the identity $\pi/(cd_m)=\theta^2/(1-\theta)$, gives <a id="nest:exact-moments"></a>$$\label{nest:exact-moments}
 \begin{split}
 M&=\frac{\theta^3}3+
       \frac{\pi(1-\theta)^2}{2cd_m}
     =\frac{\theta^2(3-\theta)}6,\\
 \int_0^1qH(q)\,dq
   &=\frac{\theta^4}4+
       \frac{\pi(1-\theta)^2(1+2\theta)}{6cd_m},\\
 q_m&=\frac{2+2\theta-\theta^2}{2(3-\theta)},\qquad
 p_2=1+cd_m^2\theta^2.
 \end{split}$$ Only $3.14<\pi<22/7$ is needed to bound these expressions. The function $0.567x^2-\pi(1-x)$ increases on $[0,1]$, and $$\begin{split}
 0.567(32/37)^2-3.14(5/37)&=-\frac{73}{342250}<0,\\
 0.567(13/15)^2-(22/7)(2/15)&=\frac{3587}{525000}>0.
 \end{split}$$ It follows that $32/37<\theta<13/15$. Both $\theta^2(3-\theta)/6$ and $(2+2\theta-\theta^2)/(2(3-\theta))$ increase on $(0,1)$; for the latter, the derivative is $(\theta-2)(\theta-4)/(2(3-\theta)^2)>0$. Consequently $$M>\frac{40448}{151959}>0.2659,
 \qquad q_m>\frac{2041}{2923}>0.697,
 \qquad p_2<\frac{2723587}{2500000}<1.09.$$ This proves <a href="#nest:moment-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:moment-bounds">[nest:moment-bounds]</a>.

Here are explicit rational margins for the remaining coefficient. Put $\beta_0=0.303d_m=0.06363$. The bounds just proved imply $$\frac{0.91cM}{p_2^{3/2}}
   >\frac{0.91\cdot2.7\cdot0.2659}{1.09^{3/2}}>0.574.$$ The last inequality follows by squaring positive quantities, since $$(0.91\cdot2.7\cdot0.2659)^2
      -0.574^2\,1.09^3
      =\frac{14121304169}{10^{14}}>0.$$ Also $(1-q_m)d_m<\beta_0$. If $u=b_0$, then $u-\beta_0>1-1/3.14-0.06363>0.617$, and $$0.574(0.617)^3=0.134824054862>0.134.$$ Hence the left side of <a href="#nest:coefficient-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:coefficient-inequality">[nest:coefficient-inequality]</a> exceeds $0.134h^3$. If $u=0.96/h$, then $$h(u-\beta_0)\geq0.96-1.62\beta_0
     =0.8569194>0.856,
 \qquad
 0.574(0.856)^3=0.360025437184>0.36.$$ Both branches therefore dominate $C_0(h)$, including their common endpoint and the switch in the definition of $C_0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-nest-area-bound"></a>
<div id="nest:area-bound" class="proposition">

<a id="nest:gain-definition"></a>
<a id="nest:area-gain"></a>
**Proposition 20** (The nesting area bound). *For the three-vertex configuration at $V=1/\pi$ with $A\leq1$, $$A-1\geq B(h)\qquad(0.64\leq h\leq2.25).$$ On $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$ define $$\label{nest:gain-definition}
 S(h)=\inf_{y\geq0}
 \left\{\ell y^2+
   C_0(h)\min\{d_m,(P-Qy)_+\}^{\,3}\right\}.$$ Then $$\label{nest:area-gain}
 A-1\geq B(h)+S(h).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-area-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-area-bound</summary>

<a id="nest:pointwise-gain"></a>
*Proof.* The first assertion follows from <a href="#nest:baseline-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:baseline-slack">[nest:baseline-slack]</a>. For the second, first suppose $\delta>0$ and restrict to the area interval $[u-\delta,u]$ of Lemma <a href="#nest:shape-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:shape-gain">17</a>. Put $K=c\delta^2H(q)$. Since $p\geq\sqrt{1+K}$ and $G$ is non-increasing, $$G(hv/p)\geq G\!\left(\frac{hv}{\sqrt{1+K}}\right).$$ On this interval the baseline uses $p_*=1$, and $hv\leq hu\leq0.96<D$. Thus the whole squared-argument interval $[(hv)^2/(1+K),(hv)^2]$ lies in the differentiable branch of $G$. By <a href="#nest:weight-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:weight-derivative">[nest:weight-derivative]</a> and Lemma <a href="#nest:derivative-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:derivative-bound">18</a>, $$\label{nest:pointwise-gain}
 \begin{split}
 G\!\left(\frac{hv}{\sqrt{1+K}}\right)-G(hv)
 &\geq \int_{(hv)^2/(1+K)}^{(hv)^2}0.91\sqrt r\,dr\\
 &\geq\frac{0.91(hv)^3K}{(1+K)^{3/2}}
 \geq\frac{0.91(hv)^3c\delta^2H(q)}{p_2^{3/2}}.
 \end{split}$$ The second inequality bounds $\sqrt r$ below by its value at the left endpoint, and the last uses $1+K\leq p_2$.

Changing variables $v=u-\delta+\delta q$ supplies a further factor $\delta$. Jensen’s inequality for the convex function $v^3$ on $v\geq0$, with probability density $H/M$, gives $$\begin{split}
 \int_0^1 (u-\delta+\delta q)^3H(q)\,dq
 &\geq M\bigl(u-(1-q_m)\delta\bigr)^3\\
 &\geq M\bigl(u-(1-q_m)d_m\bigr)^3.
 \end{split}$$ All bases are positive by <a href="#nest:interval-placement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:interval-placement">[nest:interval-placement]</a>. Integrating <a href="#nest:pointwise-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:pointwise-gain">[nest:pointwise-gain]</a> and applying Lemma <a href="#nest:coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:coefficient-bound">19</a> therefore gives an additional area contribution at least $C_0\delta^3$ beyond the baseline. If $\delta=0$, that conclusion holds trivially with zero gain; no variable $q$ is then introduced.

Together with the baseline contribution from $[t,s]$, this proves $$A-1\geq B(h)+\ell(s-t)+
 C_0\min\{d_m,(P-Q\sqrt{s-t})_+\}^{\,3}.$$ Taking the infimum over all $y\geq0$ weakens this inequality and yields <a href="#nest:area-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:area-gain">[nest:area-gain]</a>. ◻

</details>

Lemma <a href="#nest:s-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:s-formula">26</a> in Appendix <a href="#num:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:section">9</a> gives an explicit formula for the scalar infimum $S$.

<a id="completion:section"></a>
### Completion of the proof

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-area-bound-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-area-bound</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>.* By Proposition <a href="#pre:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:reduction">5</a>, it suffices to exclude nonstandard minimizers at $V_1,V_2$ in the normalized period-two torus. Proposition <a href="#geo:patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:patterns">8</a> leaves the three-vertex and four-vertex patterns. Proposition <a href="#slice:four-vertex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="slice:four-vertex">11</a> excludes the four-vertex pattern at both volumes, and Proposition <a href="#slice:three-first" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="slice:three-first">12</a> excludes the three-vertex pattern at $V_1$.

Suppose the latter pattern occurs at $V_2$. Its occupied-side choice and Proposition <a href="#slice:three-second-parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="slice:three-second-parameters">13</a> give $h\in[.64,2.25]$ with $A\le1$. Proposition <a href="#nest:area-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:area-bound">20</a> gives $$A-1\ge
 \begin{cases}
 B(h)+S(h),&1.02\le h\le1.62,\\
 B(h),&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ Proposition <a href="#num:scalar-positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:scalar-positive">22</a> makes the right-hand side strictly positive throughout the required interval, contradicting $A\le1$.

The two-volume reduction and Lemma <a href="#pre:standard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:standard">3</a> now give all volumes and all equality cases. Returning to the unit torus multiplies normalized area by two and divides lengths by two. This yields <a href="#intro:profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:profile">[intro:profile]</a> and the radii and widths stated in the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="num:elementary-estimates"></a>
### An elementary estimate for the four-vertex pattern

The endpoint flux and projection identities reduce the four-vertex configuration to the following inequality. Its variables are scalars; no geometric assumptions beyond their displayed bounds are needed.

<a id="stmt-num-four-vertex-scalar"></a>
<div id="num:four-vertex-scalar" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *Suppose $$\begin{gathered}
 0<d\le\frac1{\sqrt3},\qquad
 0\le x\le\sqrt\pi,\qquad 0\le\tau<1,\\
 \tau+\frac{x}{\pi}(1-\tau^2)=1-d^2.
 \end{gathered}$$ Then $$d+\frac{\tau^2+(\tau+xd)^2}{\pi}<1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-num-four-vertex-scalar"><summary>Proof of stmt-num-four-vertex-scalar</summary>

*Proof.* Write $R(\tau,d)$ for the expression to be bounded, with $x=\pi(1-\tau-d^2)/(1-\tau^2)$. In particular $\tau\le1-d^2$. Let $f(q)=q+x(1-q^2)/\pi$. Suppose for contradiction that $R(\tau,d)\ge1$. All terminating decimals below denote exact rational numbers; the elementary bounds $157/50<\pi<22/7$ suffice.

First, $\tau>1/10$. Otherwise the increasing function $q+(1-q^2)/\sqrt\pi$ on $[0,1/10]$ would give $$\frac23\le f(\tau)
 \le\frac1{10}+\frac{99}{100\sqrt\pi}<\frac23.$$ For the last inequality, square the positive terms and use $(99/100)^2/(157/50)<(17/30)^2$.

*The range $1/10<\tau\le3/10$.* Hold $\tau$ fixed and increase $d$ to $1/\sqrt3$, redefining $x$ by the formula above. Along this comparison $x$ decreases and remains nonnegative, because $1-\tau-d^2\ge1-3/10-1/3>0$. Thus $x\le\sqrt\pi$ remains valid. Direct differentiation gives $$\frac{\partial R}{\partial d}
 =1+\frac{2(\tau+xd)(1-\tau-3d^2)}{1-\tau^2}.$$ If the numerator’s last factor is nonnegative this derivative is at least one. Otherwise use $1-\tau-3d^2\ge-\tau$, $1-\tau^2\ge91/100$, and $$\tau+xd\le\frac3{10}+\sqrt{\frac\pi3}
 <\frac3{10}+\frac{41}{40}=\frac{53}{40}$$ to obtain $$\frac{\partial R}{\partial d}
 >1-\frac{2(3/10)(53/40)}{91/100}
 =\frac{23}{182}>0.$$ It remains to bound $R(\tau,1/\sqrt3)$. Set $$J(\tau)=\tau+
 \frac{\pi(2/3-\tau)}{\sqrt3(1-\tau^2)}.$$ On $0\le\tau\le3/10$, $$\begin{aligned}
 &1+\tau^2-\frac43\tau-\frac45(1-\tau^2)^2\\
 &\hspace{1cm}=
 \frac15-\frac43\tau+\frac{13}{5}\tau^2-\frac45\tau^4
 \ge\frac15-\frac43\tau+\frac{316}{125}\tau^2>0.
\end{aligned}$$ The last quadratic has positive leading coefficient and discriminant $-1376/5625$. Hence $$J'(\tau)
 =1-\frac\pi{\sqrt3}
     \frac{1+\tau^2-4\tau/3}{(1-\tau^2)^2}
 <1-\frac45\frac95=-\frac{11}{25},$$ where $\pi/\sqrt3>9/5$ follows by squaring. Also $$J(3/10)=\frac3{10}+\frac{110\pi}{273\sqrt3}
 >\frac3{10}+\frac{66}{91}>1.$$ Thus $J>1$ throughout this range and $(J^2+\tau^2)'<-22/25+3/5<0$. For an exact endpoint bound, use $1/\sqrt3<26/45$ to get $$J(1/10)
 <\frac1{10}+\frac{22}{7}\frac{26}{45}\frac{170}{297}
 =\frac{19381}{17010}<\frac{57}{50}.$$ It follows that $$R(\tau,d)
 \le R(1/10,1/\sqrt3)
 <\frac{26}{45}
   +\frac{1/100+(57/50)^2}{157/50}
 =\frac{35143}{35325}<1.$$

<a id="slice:four-rescaled"></a>
*The range $3/10\le\tau<1$.* Put $U=\sqrt{1-\tau}$ and $Z=d/U$. The inequality $d^2\le1-\tau$ gives $0\le Z\le1$, while $0<U\le\sqrt{7/10}$. The formula for $x$ becomes $x=\pi(1-Z^2)/(1+\tau)$, and the contradictory assumption $R(\tau,d)\ge1$ becomes $$\label{slice:four-rescaled}
 \frac{1-2\tau^2/\pi}{\sqrt{1-\tau}}
 \le Z+\frac{2\tau}{1+\tau}Z(1-Z^2)
       +\frac{\pi U}{(1+\tau)^2}Z^2(1-Z^2)^2.$$ The derivative of the left side is $$\frac{1/2+(3\tau^2-4\tau)/\pi}{(1-\tau)^{3/2}}>0,$$ because $3\tau^2-4\tau\ge-4/3$ and $\pi>8/3$. Using $\sqrt{7/10}<837/1000$, its value at $3/10$ exceeds $$\frac{148000}{131409}>
 \frac98=1.125.$$

<a id="slice:four-coefficient"></a>
To bound the right side, put $c_*=2\pi/(3\sqrt3)$. We claim that $$\label{slice:four-coefficient}
 \frac{2\tau}{1+\tau}
 +\frac{2\pi U}{3\sqrt3(1+\tau)^2}<\frac{111}{100}.$$ Multiplication by $(1+\tau)^2=(2-U^2)^2$ shows that this is equivalent to positivity of $$\frac{11}{25}-c_*U+\frac{39}{25}U^2-\frac{89}{100}U^4
 \ge\frac{11}{25}-c_*U+\frac{937}{1000}U^2.$$ The last quadratic is positive for every real $U$: its leading coefficient is positive and its discriminant satisfies $$c_*^2-4\frac{11}{25}\frac{937}{1000}
 <\frac{1936}{1323}-\frac{10307}{6250}
 =-\frac{1536161}{8268750}<0.$$ This proves <a href="#slice:four-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-coefficient">[slice:four-coefficient]</a>. Since $0\le Z(1-Z^2)\le2/(3\sqrt3)$, the right side of <a href="#slice:four-rescaled" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-rescaled">[slice:four-rescaled]</a> is at most $$Z+\frac{111}{100}Z(1-Z^2).$$ Its maximum on $[0,1]$ is $$\frac{211}{150}\sqrt{\frac{211}{333}}<\frac98,$$ as follows by squaring from $$\frac{81}{64}-\frac{9393931}{7492500}
 =\frac{1420229}{119880000}>0.$$ This contradicts <a href="#slice:four-rescaled" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slice:four-rescaled">[slice:four-rescaled]</a> and completes both ranges. The case $x=0$ was included: then $\tau=1-d^2\in[2/3,1)$, in the second range. ◻

</details>

<a id="num:section"></a>
### Certification of the scalar inequalities

We retain the definitions of $G$, $B$, and $S$ in <a href="#nest:weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:weight">[nest:weight]</a>, <a href="#nest:baseline" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:baseline">[nest:baseline]</a>, and <a href="#nest:gain-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:gain-definition">[nest:gain-definition]</a>. All terminating decimals in this appendix denote exact rational numbers. In particular, the tabulated bounds below are rational inequalities.

<a id="stmt-num-scalar-positive"></a>
<div id="num:scalar-positive" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22**. *The following strict inequalities hold: $$\begin{aligned}
 B(h)&>0 &&\text{for }h\in[.64,1.02]\cup[1.62,2.25],\\
 B(h)+S(h)&>0 &&\text{for }h\in[1.02,1.62].
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

Finite knot bounds imply positivity on the whole curvature interval through upper second derivative bounds that also control changes of formula. The proof has three parts: a useful integral representation, those derivative bounds, and finite rational certificates for interpolation.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Layer cake and the baseline curvature bound

<a id="num:rho"></a>
<a id="num:weight-density"></a>
Let $\rho$ be the inverse of the increasing function $r\mapsto r/\sqrt{\pi(1-r)}$ on $[0,1)$. Explicitly, with $\sigma=\pi/4$, $$\label{num:rho}
 \rho(k)=\sqrt\pi\,k\sqrt{1+\sigma k^2}-\frac\pi2 k^2,
 \qquad k\ge0.$$ Thus $t=\rho(1/h)$. On $[0,D]$ put $W(X)=-G'(X)$. From $$\label{num:weight-density}
 G(X)=\lambda\sqrt{1-X^2}+\frac{1-\lambda}{\sqrt{1-X^2}},
 \qquad
 W(X)=\frac{X(.96-.98X^2)}{(1-X^2)^{3/2}},$$ we have $W\ge0$ and $\int_0^D W=1-\ell=.72$.

<a id="stmt-num-layer-cake"></a>
<div id="num:layer-cake" class="lemma">

<a id="num:layer-cake-formula"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *For $.64\le h\le2.25$, $$\label{num:layer-cake-formula}
 B(h)+1=\frac{\lambda h}{\pi}+\ell t+
 \int_0^D W(X)
 \min\left\{\pi(X/h)^2,\ X/h,\ \rho(X/h)\right\}\,dX.$$ On an interval $[a,b]\subset[.64,2.25]$, the function $B(h)-h^2/a^2$ is concave.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-num-layer-cake"><summary>Proof of stmt-num-layer-cake</summary>

*Proof.* The reciprocal of the planar profile gives the increasing function $$q_h(v):=\frac{hv}{p_*(v)}=
 \max\left\{h\sqrt{v/\pi},\ hv,
             \frac{hv}{\sqrt{\pi(1-v)}}\right\}.$$ For $0\le X\le D<1$, its sublevel set in $[0,t]$ has length $$\min\{\pi(X/h)^2,X/h,\rho(X/h)\}.$$ Indeed these are the three separate sublevel restrictions; the additional restriction $v\le t$ is redundant since $\rho(X/h)<\rho(1/h)=t$. As a function of $k=X/h$, the selected branches are $\pi k^2$ for $k\le a_0$, $k$ for $a_0\le k\le b_0$, and $\rho(k)$ for $k\ge b_0$. This is also obtained by inverting the three successive pieces of $p_*$. The identity $$G(q)=\ell+\int_0^D W(X)\mathbf 1_{\{q\le X\}}\,dX
 \qquad(q\ge0)$$ and Tonelli’s theorem now prove <a href="#num:layer-cake-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:layer-cake-formula">[num:layer-cake-formula]</a>.

The function inverted to obtain $\rho$ has positive first and second derivatives. Hence $\rho$ is increasing and concave, with $\rho(0)=0$, and $k\rho'(k)\le\rho(k)\le1$. Consequently $$\frac{d^2}{dh^2}\rho(X/h)
 =\frac{k^2\rho''(k)+2k\rho'(k)}{h^2}\le\frac2{h^2}.$$ The same bound holds for $t=\rho(1/h)$. On the *selected* quadratic piece, $k\le a_0$, so $$\frac{d^2}{dh^2}(\pi k^2)=\frac{6\pi k^2}{h^2}
 \le\frac6{\pi h^2}<\frac2{h^2}.$$ On the selected linear piece the second derivative is $2k/h^2<2/h^2$, since $k\le b_0<1$.

These bounds concern only the pieces that are actually selected. For each fixed $X>0$, as $h$ increases they occur in the order $\rho(X/h)$, $X/h$, $\pi(X/h)^2$. At the first change, $\rho'(b_0)=2/(\pi+1)<1$, so the first derivative decreases. At the second it changes from $-a_0/h$ to $-2a_0/h$, again decreasing. There is thus no positive atom in the distributional second derivative. The case $X=0$ is the constant zero function. Each integrand, and also $t$, becomes concave on $[a,b]$ after subtracting $h^2/a^2$. Their nonnegative weights in <a href="#num:layer-cake-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:layer-cake-formula">[num:layer-cake-formula]</a> sum to $\ell+.72=1$. The remaining term is affine, proving the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The upper second derivative bound for the gain

We say that a function has upper second derivative bound $K$ on an interval if subtracting $Kh^2/2$ makes it concave there. This convention includes changes of formula and does not require twice differentiability.

<a id="stmt-num-s-second"></a>
<div id="num:s-second" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24**. *The function $S$ has upper second derivative bound $.4$ on $[1.02,1.62]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-num-s-second"><summary>Proof of stmt-num-s-second</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $y\ge0$, and write $$F_y(h)=\ell y^2+C_0(h)f(w(h)),\qquad
 f(w)=\min\{d_m,w_+\}^3,
 \quad w=u-1+R-Qy,\quad R=2ht/\pi.$$ Then $S=\inf_{y\ge0}F_y$. Differentiation of $h^2t^2=\pi(1-t)$ gives <a id="num:r-q-derivatives"></a>$$\label{num:r-q-derivatives}
 \begin{gathered}
 R'=\frac{2t^2}{\pi(2-t)},\quad R''\le0,
 \qquad Q=\frac2{\sqrt\pi}(1+4h^2/\pi)^{1/4},\\
 \frac{Q'}Q=\frac{2h}{\pi+4h^2},\qquad
 \frac{Q''}Q=\frac{2\pi-4h^2}{(\pi+4h^2)^2}.
 \end{gathered}$$ In particular, $0<Q'/Q\le1/(2h)$ and $Q''/Q\ge-1/(4h^2)$.

Here are rational bounds sufficient for the argument. The inequalities $3.14<\pi<22/7$ and the equation for $t$ imply $$.64<t<.8,\qquad t(1.62)<.65.$$ For example these follow by substituting the proposed endpoints into $h^2t^2-\pi(1-t)$, which is strictly increasing in $t$. Since $t$ decreases and $R$ increases, they yield $$.19<\frac{1792}{9350}<R'
 <\frac{160}{471}<.34,
 \qquad
 R\le R(1.62)<\frac{1053}{1570}<.671.$$ On a piece where $0<w<d_m$, it follows that $$0\le Qy<P\le b_0-1+R<.36.$$ The switch in $u=\min\{b_0,.96/h\}$ occurs at $h_c=.96/b_0>1.4$. On its constant piece, $$w'>.19-\frac{.36}{2(1.02)}>0,
 \qquad w''\le\frac{.36}{4(1.02)^2}<.8.$$ On its reciprocal piece, $$w'>-\frac{.96}{1.4^2}+.19-\frac{.36}{2(1.4)}>-.50,
 \qquad
 w''\le\frac{1.92}{1.4^3}+\frac{.36}{4(1.4)^2}<.8.$$ On both pieces $w'\le R'<.34$. Also, on every smooth piece, $$0<C_0\le.36,\qquad 0\le C_0'\le1.08,
 \qquad 0\le C_0''\le2.16;$$ these follow directly from $C_0=\min\{.134h^3,.36\}$ and $h\le1.62$. Thus on an active cubic piece the product rule gives $$\begin{aligned}
 (C_0w^3)''
 &=C_0''w^3+6C_0'w^2w'+6C_0w(w')^2+3C_0w^2w''\\
 &\le2.16d_m^3+6(1.08)d_m^2(.34)
       +6(.36)d_m(.50)^2+3(.36)d_m^2(.8)\\
 &=.26866728<.4.
\end{aligned}$$ On $w<0$ the added term is zero; on $w>d_m$ its second derivative is at most $2.16d_m^3<.4$.

For completeness, all possible simultaneous changes of formula preserve this bound. The only jumps in $w'$ and $C_0'$ are nonpositive, coming respectively from the minima defining $u$ and $C_0$. At a point with $0<w<d_m$ the jump in $F_y'$ is $$w^3\Delta C_0'+3C_0w^2\Delta w'\le0.$$ At $w=0$, both $f$ and its derivative vanish, so the derivatives match even at a simultaneous switch. At $w=d_m$, the one-sided derivatives of $f(w(h))$ are $$3d_m^2\max\{w'_-,0\}\quad\hbox{from the left},\qquad
 3d_m^2\min\{w'_+,0\}\quad\hbox{from the right}.$$ Their difference is nonpositive; a simultaneous switch of $C_0$ adds $d_m^3\Delta C_0'\le0$. These formulas include tangencies. On a capped piece only the nonpositive jump of $C_0'$ remains. For fixed $y$ these functions are algebraic on each branch, so the threshold points are finite unless a threshold holds identically on a piece; an identical threshold has the matching derivatives just described. It follows that $F_y-.2h^2$ is concave on the full interval. Finally, $$S(h)-.2h^2=\inf_{y\ge0}\bigl(F_y(h)-.2h^2\bigr)$$ is concave: the pointwise infimum of concave functions is concave, as is seen either from its defining inequality or its hypograph. The infimum is finite, since $F_y\ge0$ and $F_0\le C_0d_m^3$. This also handles changes of the minimizing value of $y$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Reduction of the knot values to arithmetic

Only the factor $\rho(X/h)$ in <a href="#num:layer-cake-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:layer-cake-formula">[num:layer-cake-formula]</a> will require polynomial approximation. Set $L=(D/h-b_0)_+$. If $L>0$, replace $\rho$ on $[b_0,D/h]$ by its cubic Taylor polynomial about $k_*=(b_0+D/h)/2$, without changing either branch endpoint. Denote the resulting value of $B$ by $\widetilde B$. If $L=0$, put $\widetilde B=B$.

<a id="stmt-num-taylor-error"></a>
<div id="num:taylor-error" class="lemma">

<a id="num:taylor-bound"></a>
**Lemma 25**. *For $.64\le h\le2.25$, $$\label{num:taylor-bound}
 B(h)\ge\widetilde B(h)-.008(D/h-b_0)_+^4.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-num-taylor-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-num-taylor-error</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiating <a href="#num:rho" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:rho">[num:rho]</a> gives $$\rho'''(k)=\frac{3\sqrt\pi\,\sigma}{(1+\sigma k^2)^{5/2}},
 \qquad
 \rho''''(k)=-\frac{15\sqrt\pi\,\sigma^2 k}
                         {(1+\sigma k^2)^{7/2}}.$$ The magnitude of the latter expression decreases when $6\sigma k^2\ge1$, in particular for $k\ge b_0$. Since $.68<b_0<.69$ and $3.14<\pi<22/7$, $$|\rho''''(b_0)|
 <\frac{15\sqrt{22/7}(11/14)^2(.69)}
          {(1+(3.14/4)(.68)^2)^{7/2}}<4.$$ The final inequality follows by squaring positive quantities and cross-multiplying rational numbers. The midpoint Taylor remainder is therefore at most $4(L/2)^4/24$ in absolute value. Multiplying by the total weight $.72$ bounds its contribution to the integral by $.0075L^4\le.008L^4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="num:moments-l"></a>
<a id="num:moments-j"></a>
Here are explicit moment formulas to evaluate $\widetilde B$. For $0\le z\le D$, define $$L_n(z)=\int_0^z\frac{X^n}{\sqrt{1-X^2}}\,dX,
 \qquad J_j(z)=\int_0^z X^jW(X)\,dX.$$ Writing $\beta_z=\sqrt{1-z^2}$, integration by parts gives $$\label{num:moments-l}
 \begin{gathered}
 L_0(z)=\arcsin z,\qquad L_1(z)=1-\beta_z,\\
 L_n(z)=\frac{(n-1)L_{n-2}(z)-z^{n-1}\beta_z}{n}\qquad(n\ge2).
 \end{gathered}$$ $$\label{num:moments-j}
 J_j(z)=\lambda L_{j+1}(z)
 -(1-\lambda)\left(\frac{z^j}{\beta_z}
       -\mathbf1_{\{j=0\}}-jL_{j-1}(z)\right),$$ where the term $jL_{j-1}$ is omitted for $j=0$. Only $0\le j\le3$ and $0\le n\le4$ are needed. To check <a href="#num:moments-j" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:moments-j">[num:moments-j]</a>, use <a href="#num:weight-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:weight-density">[num:weight-density]</a> and differentiate $X^j/\sqrt{1-X^2}$; the indicator is its boundary term at zero.

<a id="num:evaluation-b"></a>
Put $x=ha_0$ and $y=\min\{D,hb_0\}$. Throughout the stated range, $0<x\le y\le D$ and $x<D$. If the Taylor polynomial is $\sum_{j=0}^3c_jk^j$, the evaluation formula is $$\label{num:evaluation-b}
 \widetilde B=\frac{\lambda h}{\pi}+\ell t-1
  +\frac\pi{h^2}J_2(x)+\frac{J_1(y)-J_1(x)}h
  +\sum_{j=0}^3\frac{c_j}{h^j}\bigl(J_j(D)-J_j(y)\bigr).$$ The sum is omitted when $y=D$. Otherwise, put $r_j=\rho^{(j)}(k_*)/j!$. Explicitly, $$\begin{aligned}
 r_0&=\sqrt\pi\,k_*\sqrt{1+\sigma k_*^2}-\pi k_*^2/2,\\
 r_1&=\frac{\sqrt\pi(1+2\sigma k_*^2)}{\sqrt{1+\sigma k_*^2}}
       -\pi k_*,\\
 r_2&=\frac12\left(
 \frac{\sqrt\pi\,\sigma k_*(3+2\sigma k_*^2)}
      {(1+\sigma k_*^2)^{3/2}}-\pi\right),
 &r_3&=\frac{\sqrt\pi\,\sigma}{2(1+\sigma k_*^2)^{5/2}},\\
 c_0&=r_0-k_*r_1+k_*^2r_2-k_*^3r_3,
 &c_1&=r_1-2k_*r_2+3k_*^2r_3,\\
 c_2&=r_2-3k_*r_3,&c_3&=r_3.
\end{aligned}$$ The gain $S$ also has an evaluation formula using only arithmetic and square roots.

<a id="stmt-nest-s-formula"></a>
<div id="nest:s-formula" class="lemma">

<a id="nest:s-critical-point"></a>
<a id="nest:s-explicit"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Evaluation of the capped gain). *On $1.02\leq h\leq1.62$, $P,Q,C_0$ are positive. With $$\label{nest:s-critical-point}
 q_*=\frac{2P}{1+\sqrt{1+6C_0Q^2P/\ell}},$$ one has, including every cap and zero case, $$\label{nest:s-explicit}
 S(h)=\min\left\{C_0d_m^3,
       \ell\left(\frac{P-q_*}{Q}\right)^2+C_0q_*^3\right\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-s-formula"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-s-formula</summary>

*Proof.* Positivity of $Q,C_0$ is immediate. To see that $P>0$ uniformly, note that $t(h)$ decreases, so $ht(h)=\sqrt{\pi(1-t(h))}$ increases. At $h=1$ the equation for $t$ gives $t(1)>3/4$, since $9/16-\pi/4<0$. Using $h\geq1$, $u\geq16/27$, and $\pi<22/7$, we get $$P>\frac{16}{27}-1+\frac{21}{44}
   =\frac{83}{1188}>0.$$

For a fixed $h$, write $w=P-Qy$, so $w\leq P$. Define the uncapped polynomial objective on $[0,P]$ by $$F(w)=\frac{\ell(P-w)^2}{Q^2}+C_0w^3.$$ It agrees with the original objective on the feasible uncapped interval $[0,\min\{P,d_m\}]$. The polynomial is strictly convex on $[0,P]$, with $F'(0)<0<F'(P)$, and its unique critical point lies in $(0,P)$. Solving $3C_0Q^2w^2=2\ell(P-w)$ gives exactly $w=q_*$ in <a href="#nest:s-critical-point" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:s-critical-point">[nest:s-critical-point]</a>.

The branch $w\leq0$ has minimum $\ell P^2/Q^2=F(0)$, which is greater than $F(q_*)$. If $P\geq d_m$, the capped branch $d_m\leq w\leq P$ has minimum $C_0d_m^3$, attained at $w=P$. When $q_*\leq d_m$ both candidates in <a href="#nest:s-explicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:s-explicit">[nest:s-explicit]</a> are consequently sufficient. When $q_*>d_m$, the minimum of $F$ on $[0,d_m]$ is $F(d_m)$, which is at least $C_0d_m^3$; moreover $F(q_*)\geq C_0q_*^3>C_0d_m^3$. Thus the capped value still wins in the displayed formula even though $q_*$ is infeasible. Finally, if $P<d_m$, the cap is never reached, but its displayed value is greater than $C_0P^3=F(P)\geq F(q_*)$ and hence cannot alter the minimum. The equalities $P=d_m$ and $q_*=d_m$ are included in these comparisons. This proves the formula in all cases. ◻

</details>

<a id="num:arithmetic"></a>
#### An explicit outward arithmetic prescription

<a id="num:pi-interval"></a>
We specify the arithmetic behind the table so that each entry can be verified with integer operations. The input enclosure for $\pi$ is $$\label{num:pi-interval}
 \pi_-:=3.14159265358979<\pi<3.14159265358980=:\pi_+.$$ Here is a rational verification of that enclosure. Set $$A_N(z)=\sum_{j=0}^N\frac{(-1)^jz^{2j+1}}{2j+1},
 \qquad e_N(z)=\frac{z^{2N+3}}{2N+3}.$$ For odd $N$ and $0<z<1$, the alternating series gives $A_N(z)<\arctan z<A_N(z)+e_N(z)$. Machin’s identity $\pi=16\arctan(1/5)-4\arctan(1/239)$ follows by the tangent addition formula, with the angle in $(0,\pi/2)$. Thus the following entirely rational comparisons prove <a href="#num:pi-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:pi-interval">[num:pi-interval]</a>: $$\begin{aligned}
 \pi_-&<16A_{31}(1/5)-4\bigl(A_9(1/239)+e_9(1/239)\bigr),\\
 16\bigl(A_{31}(1/5)+e_{31}(1/5)\bigr)-4A_9(1/239)&<\pi_+.
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="num:arcsine-enclosure"></a>
The arcsine in <a href="#num:moments-l" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:moments-l">[num:moments-l]</a> can likewise be enclosed without a transcendental evaluation. Define $$m(z)=\frac{z}{\sqrt{2+2\sqrt{1-z^2}}},\qquad
 j_z=m(m(z)),\qquad
 T(j)=\sum_{n=0}^{10}\frac{\binom{2n}{n}}{4^n(2n+1)}j^{2n+1}.$$ Twice applying the half-angle identity gives $\arcsin z=4\arcsin j_z$. The function $m$ is increasing on $[0,D]$, and $j_D^2=(1-2/\sqrt7)/2<1/8<.36^2$, so $j_z<.36$. The positive arcsine-series coefficients are at most one. Hence $$\label{num:arcsine-enclosure}
 4T(j_z)\le\arcsin z
 \le4T(j_z)+\frac{4j_z^{23}}{1-j_z^2}.$$

For a precise common rounding rule, take $N=10^{40}$ and represent an interval by integer endpoints $[a,b]_N=[a/N,b/N]$. Enclose each rational input $r$ by $[\lfloor Nr\rfloor,\lceil Nr\rceil]_N$. Addition and negation are exact at this scale. For multiplication use $$[a,b]_N[c,d]_N\subseteq
 \left[\left\lfloor\frac{\min\{ac,ad,bc,bd\}}N\right\rfloor,
       \left\lceil\frac{\max\{ac,ad,bc,bd\}}N\right\rceil\right]_N.$$ For $0<a\le b$, reciprocal is enclosed by $[\lfloor N^2/b\rfloor,\lceil N^2/a\rceil]_N$; negative intervals are treated by negation. Division is multiplication by this reciprocal. For $0\le a\le b$, square root is enclosed by $$[\lfloor\sqrt{aN}\rfloor,\lceil\sqrt{bN}\rceil]_N,$$ whose endpoints are found by integer-square comparisons. The zeroth power is $[N,N]_N$. An odd nonnegative integer power takes its extrema at the endpoints; an even positive power has lower endpoint zero if the interval contains zero, and otherwise the smaller endpoint power. Divide those integer powers by $N^{n-1}$ and round outward. For minimum and maximum, apply the same operation separately to the two lower and the two upper endpoints. Each rule encloses every possible value of its operation, also when its inputs are dependent. Induction therefore proves the enclosure property for any expression formed by these rules.

Apply these rules to <a href="#num:evaluation-b" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:evaluation-b">[num:evaluation-b]</a> in the following order: $\pi$, $a_0,b_0,D,t,x,y$; then $j_z$ and <a href="#num:arcsine-enclosure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:arcsine-enclosure">[num:arcsine-enclosure]</a> for $z=x,y,D$; then the recurrences <a href="#num:moments-l" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:moments-l">[num:moments-l]</a>–<a href="#num:moments-j" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:moments-j">[num:moments-j]</a>; then $k_*,r_j,c_j$ if $hb_0<D$; and finally <a href="#num:evaluation-b" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:evaluation-b">[num:evaluation-b]</a>. Integer powers are evaluated by the endpoint rule just given, and half-integer powers as integer powers times a square root. Evaluate <a href="#nest:s-explicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:s-explicit">[nest:s-explicit]</a> from $t,u,P,Q,C_0,q_*$ in that order, enclosing minima coordinatewise. No tabulated knot is a branch tie $hb_0=D$, so that branch choice is determined by disjoint intervals. In <a href="#num:arcsine-enclosure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:arcsine-enclosure">[num:arcsine-enclosure]</a> retain the lower endpoint of $4T$ and the upper endpoint of $4T+4j_z^{23}/(1-j_z^2)$.

<div id="num:knot-table">

|  $h$ | $b_h$ | $s_h$ |  $h$ | $b_h$ | $s_h$ |
|-----:|------:|------:|-----:|------:|------:|
|  .64 |  1744 |     – | 1.30 |  -196 |   271 |
|  .74 |  1200 |     – | 1.34 |  -211 |   297 |
|  .84 |   735 |     – | 1.38 |  -226 |   325 |
|  .92 |   432 |     – | 1.42 |  -247 |   332 |
|  .98 |   247 |     – | 1.46 |  -275 |   332 |
| 1.02 |   144 |    76 | 1.50 |  -260 |   318 |
| 1.06 |    57 |    98 | 1.54 |  -193 |   293 |
| 1.10 |   -15 |   123 | 1.58 |   -76 |   271 |
| 1.14 |   -74 |   151 | 1.62 |    86 |   251 |
| 1.18 |  -119 |   182 | 1.70 |   536 |     – |
| 1.22 |  -153 |   215 | 1.90 |  2275 |     – |
| 1.26 |  -178 |   247 | 2.25 |  6853 |     – |

Rational lower bounds: $10^5\widetilde B(h)>b_h$ and $10^5S(h)>s_h$. A dash means that $S$ is not used.

</div>

Substitution by this prescription gives Table <a href="#num:knot-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:knot-table">1</a>. For additional checkable rounding slack, the resulting enclosures obey <a id="num:table-slack"></a>$$\label{num:table-slack}
 \begin{aligned}
 1.10&<10^5\widetilde B(h)-b_h<1.95
 &&\hbox{at all 24 knots},\\
 1.00&<10^5S(h)-s_h<1.66
 &&\hbox{at all 16 applicable knots}.
 \end{aligned}$$ These inequalities include the arcsine-series tail and every arithmetic rounding. They are deliberately coarser than the enclosures. For example at the particularly small combined value $h=1.46$ they give, more explicitly, $$-273.170<10^5\widetilde B(1.46)<-273.169,
 \qquad 10^5S(1.46)=333.396.$$ The latter equality follows from the cap term in <a href="#nest:s-explicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:s-explicit">[nest:s-explicit]</a>; here the Taylor error is zero.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Certification between every pair of knots

<a id="num:chord"></a>
If $f$ has upper second derivative bound $K$ on $[a,b]$, concavity of $f-Kh^2/2$ implies $$\label{num:chord}
 f(h)\ge\frac{b-h}{b-a}f(a)+\frac{h-a}{b-a}f(b)
             -\frac K2(h-a)(b-h)
 \ge\min\{f(a),f(b)\}-\frac{K(b-a)^2}{8}.$$ By Lemmas <a href="#num:layer-cake" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:layer-cake">23</a> and <a href="#num:s-second" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:s-second">24</a>, use $K=2/a^2$ for an exterior interval and $K=2.4$ for an interior interval: indeed $2/(1.02)^2+.4<2.4$.

<a id="num:gap-formula"></a>
To make the final comparisons rational as well, set $$D_+=.989743318611,\qquad
 b_-=1-1/\pi_-,\qquad
 E(h)=.008\max\{D_+/h-b_-,0\}^4.$$ The integer comparison $49D_+^2>48$ shows $D<D_+$, so $E(h)$ is an upper bound for the error in <a href="#num:taylor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:taylor-bound">[num:taylor-bound]</a>. List the 24 knots of Table <a href="#num:knot-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:knot-table">1</a> in increasing order. For consecutive knots $a,b$ set $\chi=1$ if $[a,b]\subset[1.02,1.62]$, and $\chi=0$ otherwise. Then a rational uniform lower bound from <a href="#num:chord" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:chord">[num:chord]</a> is $$\label{num:gap-formula}
 M_{a,b}=
 \min_{h\in\{a,b\}}
       \left\{\frac{b_h+\chi s_h}{10^5}-E(h)\right\}
       -\frac{K(b-a)^2}{8},
 \qquad K=\begin{cases}2.4,&\chi=1,\\2/a^2,&\chi=0.\end{cases}$$ Here $s_h$ is omitted when $\chi=0$, including at the shared endpoints $1.02$ and $1.62$. Every entry in Table <a href="#num:gap-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:gap-table">2</a> is verified directly from <a href="#num:gap-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:gap-formula">[num:gap-formula]</a> by rational cross-multiplication. Thus it requires no integral or transcendental evaluation in addition to Table <a href="#num:knot-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:knot-table">1</a>.

<div id="num:gap-table">

| $[a,b]$       | $10^6M_{a,b}\ge$ | $[a,b]$       | $10^6M_{a,b}\ge$ |
|:--------------|-----------------:|:--------------|-----------------:|
| $[.64,.74]$   |             4416 | $[1.26,1.30]$ |              209 |
| $[.74,.84]$   |             2298 | $[1.30,1.34]$ |              269 |
| $[.84,.92]$   |             1859 | $[1.34,1.38]$ |              379 |
| $[.92,.98]$   |             1313 | $[1.38,1.42]$ |              369 |
| $[.98,1.02]$  |              967 | $[1.42,1.46]$ |               90 |
| $[1.02,1.06]$ |             1037 | $[1.46,1.50]$ |               90 |
| $[1.06,1.10]$ |              581 | $[1.50,1.54]$ |              100 |
| $[1.10,1.14]$ |              280 | $[1.54,1.58]$ |              520 |
| $[1.14,1.18]$ |              145 | $[1.58,1.62]$ |             1470 |
| $[1.18,1.22]$ |              137 | $[1.62,1.70]$ |              250 |
| $[1.22,1.26]$ |              137 | $[1.70,1.90]$ |             1899 |
|               |                  | $[1.90,2.25]$ |            14266 |

All 23 interpolation intervals. The last column of each pair is an integer lower bound for $10^6M_{a,b}$.

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-s-formula-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-s-formula</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#num:scalar-positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:scalar-positive">22</a>.* Apply <a href="#num:chord" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:chord">[num:chord]</a> with the corrected knot lower bounds in <a href="#num:gap-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="num:gap-formula">[num:gap-formula]</a>. Table <a href="#num:gap-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="num:gap-table">2</a> gives a strictly positive lower bound on every interval, and those intervals cover the stated ranges, including all endpoints. The smallest certified uniform margin is $9/100000$, on $[1.42,1.46]$ and $[1.46,1.50]$. In particular, the corrected lower bound at $1.46$ is exactly $.00057$ and the interior interpolation loss is $.00048$. ◻

</details>
